# **SEAWEEDS OF AMERICAN SAMOA**

## **EXECUTIVE SUMMARY**

This study provides the latest, up-to-date information on the diversity of marine plants (algae and seagrasses) of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 5 ---
A general introduction to marine plants is provided, with observations on the flora of the islands.

--- SENTENCE 6 ---
An illustrated guide to 67 macroalgae and seagrasses is provided in this report.

--- SENTENCE 7 ---
The surveys covered 26 sites from four inhabited islands Tutuila, Aunu'u, Ofu and Olosega and two smaller uninhabited islands – Nu'utele and Nu'usilaelae.

--- SENTENCE 8 ---
The habitats surveyed include subtidal sites to 20-m depth, inter tidal and the spray or splash zone.

--- SENTENCE 9 ---
It also included surveys of relatively pristine coral reefs as well as high impact areas such as the inner Pago Pago Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 10 ---
Two separate surveys, both over a two-week period in October 2002 and September 2003 were carried out and the final results are reported herein.

--- SENTENCE 11 ---
A total of 635 specimens were analyzed and 239 algal and seagrass species were enumerated.

--- SENTENCE 12 ---
The red algae (Rhodophyta) had the highest number of species with 133 identified.

--- SENTENCE 13 ---
The green algae (Chlorophyta) had 60 species, the brown algae (Phaeophyta) had 29, and the blue-green (Cyanophyta) 27; only two seagrass species were found.

--- SENTENCE 14 ---
This study showed that the flora of American Samoa is diverse, and that what is recorded reflects about 60% of the potential flora.

--- SENTENCE 15 ---
The most diverse site was Utulei with 56 species; a site located near the Pago Pago Harbor entrance.

--- SENTENCE 16 ---
Other notable diverse sites include the Fagatele National Marine Sanctuary with 50 species, Fagasa (44), Mafafa (36), Aua (32), and Onososopo and Fagaalu both with 31 species.

--- SENTENCE 17 ---
The least diverse sites were the docks and the smaller Nu'usilaelae Island.

--- SENTENCE 18 ---
This is attributed to the limited collections from these sites for safety reasons.

--- SENTENCE 19 ---
There are no substantiated records of endemic algae or seagrasses from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 20 ---
Two species recorded by the American phycologist, William Setchell in 1924 (*Sargassum fonanonense* and *Sargassum anapense*) need more studies to verify their endemicity, as similar species have been recorded in the neighboring Western Samoa islands, where they are known under different names.

--- SENTENCE 21 ---
Four algae are considered to be recently introduced into the American Samoan flora.

--- SENTENCE 22 ---
*Halymenia durvillei* and *Caulerpa serrulata* may have been introduced from Apia Harbor, Western Samoa, whereas *Grateloupia filicina* and *Codium mamillosum* may have been introduced from places beyond the Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 23 ---
Although only single specimens were collected for the latter two algae, it is prudent that monitoring and surveys must be maintained to keep abreast of any potential impacts.

--- SENTENCE 24 ---
The flora of American Samoa fits in with the biogeographic theory of decreasing diversity away from the Indo-Pacific centre of biodiversity.

--- SENTENCE 25 ---
The 222 species of red, green and brown algae is well above those of the Cook Islands (67) and the Easter Islands (134), but below that of French Polynesia (308).

--- SENTENCE 26 ---
The reason for this disparity is attributed to the intensity of collections.

--- SENTENCE 27 ---
The diversity of American Samoan seagrasses and algae are better known than those of neighboring island countries.

--- SENTENCE 28 ---
To allow for a continuous buildup and maintenance of this knowledge the following observations and recommendations are made.

--- SENTENCE 29 ---
#### *RECOMMENDATIONS*

#### *1.

--- SENTENCE 30 ---
Compilation of algal surveys*

A comprehensive compilation of all algal collections, literature and data specific to American Samoa needs to be undertaken.

--- SENTENCE 31 ---
There have been many itinerant algal collectors and expeditions passing through the islands, who have taken specimens to their institutions or their personal herbaria.

--- SENTENCE 32 ---
These collectors and expeditions need to be identified and a database designed to document these, so that future researchers are aware of previously undertaken work.

--- SENTENCE 33 ---
There are records of marine plants from the Archipelago being reported in the literature.

--- SENTENCE 34 ---
Some of the literature is obscure and difficult to obtain, or may be found in personal libraries.

--- SENTENCE 35 ---
A concerted effort to identify, copy and obtain these references is recommended, and that they be kept in places where local researchers can access them.

--- SENTENCE 36 ---
The need to have a dedicated database where the marine biodiversity is stored should be considered paramount.

--- SENTENCE 37 ---
It is strongly urged that authorities consider building such a database using information that has been obtained in this and previous studies.

#### *2. Field guide to American Samoan marine plants*

This report illustrates 67 species of marine plants found during the surveys.

--- SENTENCE 38 ---
This represents a small part of the overall flora of the islands.

--- SENTENCE 39 ---
It is therefore recommended that consideration be given in developing a comprehensive field guide.

--- SENTENCE 40 ---
The field guide should be in simple language, both in English and Samoan and it should have useful and interesting information capable of raising awareness of the importance of marine plants on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 41 ---
Such a field guide would be useful in the tourism industry as well as an important reference for schools.

--- SENTENCE 42 ---
Ecological monitoring*

American Samoa has been at the forefront of coral reef monitoring.

--- SENTENCE 43 ---
The well-known transect undertaken by Alfred Major in the 1920s provides vital baseline information on the reef structure.

--- SENTENCE 44 ---
Subsequent coral reef workers have used Major's transect to compare the health of the reef in the 1920s with that of today.

--- SENTENCE 45 ---
Marine plants are an important part of the coral reef ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 46 ---
They are often the dominant organisms as seen from Fagatele National Marine Sanctuary.

--- SENTENCE 47 ---
Through this survey, the data on marine plants should be useful when ecological surveys are undertaken.

--- SENTENCE 48 ---
It is important for ecologists and coral reef monitors to start monitoring the dominant species, rather than life-form categories.

--- SENTENCE 49 ---
Expert studies of problematic groups*

Coralline algae and cyanophytes are important marine plants in American Samoa, with the former contributing to reef structure and stability, and the latter important indicators of eutropihication, and also functioning as nitrogen-fixers.

--- SENTENCE 50 ---
Because of limited time, these two groups have not been thoroughly treated here.

--- SENTENCE 51 ---
Their taxonomy, in particular the latter group, remains controversial.

--- SENTENCE 52 ---
A detailed treatment of these two groups is recommended.

#### *5.

--- SENTENCE 53 ---
Eutrophication*

With increasing industrial and residential development and settlement of coastal areas, it is expected that sediment and run-off will leach towards the sea.

--- SENTENCE 54 ---
The steep landscape of American Samoa means that surface water run-off is a lot faster than on more gently-sloping coastlines.

--- SENTENCE 55 ---
Pollutants, rubbish and nutrients are also washed to the sea where they can have a serious impact on the marine life.

--- SENTENCE 56 ---
Algal blooms will increase with high nutrients in the environment.

--- SENTENCE 57 ---
Diminishing diversity of corals and other marine animals is expected.

--- SENTENCE 58 ---
It is recommended that appropriate measures be undertaken to safeguard the marine environment.

--- SENTENCE 59 ---
Coupled with these measures is the need to undertaken research in algal population and algal physiology.

--- SENTENCE 60 ---
Invasive species*

Effective and continuous monitoring is needed to detect and weed out potential invasive species.

--- SENTENCE 61 ---
This may mean establishing monitoring sites in places where invasive species are likely to be found, such as the inner Pago Pago harbor.

--- SENTENCE 62 ---
Although only a few relatively less invasive introduced species were found, the consequences of an outbreak by an invader can easily threaten the integrity of the marine environment and the livelihood of the people.

--- SENTENCE 63 ---
Measures must be taken, including developing appropriate legislation, technology and monitoring regime to keep abreast of invasive species.

--- SENTENCE 64 ---
Marine plants for food*

Marine plants, especially algae, provide an excellent source of food.

--- SENTENCE 65 ---
Only two species are commonly eaten in Samoa, although there are probably over 10 seaweed species that can be eaten.

--- SENTENCE 66 ---
A recipe book on seaweeds, published by the Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC) and the Marine Studies Programme (USP), should be encouraged to promote the consumption of seaweeds by the local people.

--- SENTENCE 67 ---
The book may need to be translated into the Samoan language to allow for wider audience participation.

--- SENTENCE 68 ---
Surveys of other sites*

There is a need for marine plant surveys of Tau Island, and Swains and Rose atolls, as these sites were not surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 69 ---
Other sites on Tutuila, Ofu, Aunu'u, Olosega islands need to be revisited so that all subtidal and high inter tidal sites are surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
This will include surveys of mangroves and brackish water habitats.

## **1.0 INTRODUCTION**

### *1.1 Background to this report*

This report specifically assesses the macro-algal and seagrass diversity of American Samoa (Figure 1).

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
The report is sponsored by the Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources (DMWR), Government of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
The field work was undertaken from September 8th to 28th, with three of the four the main islands (Tutuila, Ofu and Olosega) surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 78 ---
The smaller islands of Aunu'u, Nu'utele and Nu'usalaelae were also surveyed, albeit with limited collections due to adverse weather conditions.

--- SENTENCE 79 ---
The larger island of Tau remains to be surveyed together with the two remote atolls – Rose and Swains.

--- SENTENCE 80 ---
Logistically the latter two places would be difficult, but Tau was hampered by bad weather and technical problems.

--- SENTENCE 81 ---
Sixteen-sites were surveyed, eight on Tutuila, three on Ofu Island, two on Olosega and one each at Aunu'u, Nu'usalaelae and Nu'utele.

--- SENTENCE 82 ---
For a more holistic address of macro-algal diversity of the islands, this report builds on previous surveys carried out in October 2002 (Skelton 2002).

--- SENTENCE 83 ---
The 2002 surveys were undertaken as part of a study of introduced marine species under the auspices of the Bernice P. Bishop Museum (Coles *et al*. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 84 ---
An important component of these surveys was the sites, which were chosen based on those identified in the American Samoa Coral Reef Monitoring Plan (ASCRMP).

--- SENTENCE 85 ---
The surveys followed closely the given location in the ASCRMP, but some modifications were made due to unfavorable weather conditions and technical oversights.

--- SENTENCE 86 ---
Some collections made were beyond the identified ASCRMP sites to allow for a complete floral assessment, as algae can be habitat specific.

--- SENTENCE 87 ---
The specimens collected in these surveys are housed at the Bishop Museum (BISH), the South Pacific Regional Herbarium (SUVA-A) and DMWR.

--- SENTENCE 88 ---
In this report, an introduction to marine plants is provided and specifically targets the macroalgae and seagrasses, the two main groups of plants that are often encountered along the shallow shores of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 89 ---
A brief overview of historical collections of American Samoa beginning from the early 1920s is provided.

--- SENTENCE 90 ---
An illustrated field guide of 70 common and interesting algae and seagrasses is also provided, each with a brief description.

--- SENTENCE 91 ---
Finally, the report discusses some interesting observations warranted after the surveys.

## *1.2 Marine plants*

Until about 450 million years ago, all plants were marine plants.

--- SENTENCE 92 ---
They are the primary producers of the seas.

--- SENTENCE 93 ---
They range from small one-celled plants to large trees (e.g. mangroves).

--- SENTENCE 94 ---
Despite this simplified understanding of plants, the debate still remains on what should or should not be a plant.

--- SENTENCE 95 ---
For the purpose of this report, the marine

plants discussed here cover five phyla or divisions: Rhodophyta, Phaeophyta, Chlorophyta, Cyanophyta and Magnoliophyta.

--- SENTENCE 96 ---
The first four phyla are generally grouped as algae, although they belong to two different kingdoms (Plantae and Monera).

--- SENTENCE 97 ---
The phylum Magnoliophyta includes all the 50 species of seagrasses, of which only two are recorded here.

!

--- SENTENCE 98 ---
Map of Tutuila, Ofu and Olosega, American Samoa, indicating the collecting sites.

--- SENTENCE 99 ---
#### **Algae**

Algae are ubiquitous diverse plants that are found from deserts, the atmosphere, snow-covered mountains, and tropical rainforests, to coastal shores and oceans.

--- SENTENCE 100 ---
What most are familiar with when visiting the beach are the seaweeds, either washed up on the beach or perched conspicuously on the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 101 ---
Others may notice that on occasion a colored soup-like film floating around calm waters, instinctively followed by a strong stench known as red-tides; these are made up of another group of algae.

--- SENTENCE 102 ---
Algae comprise about 90% of all marine plants.

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
They are simple in their structure but have quite interesting yet complex life histories and equally complex reproductive systems, as illustrated in Figure 2-4.

#### *Chlorophyta* **(green algae)**

The green alga *Ulva* (a diagrammatic representation given in Figure 2) is closely related to *Enteromorpha*, of which *Enteromorpha intestinalis* is reported herein.

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
The latest information suggests that the two genera should be merged and that the older name *Ulva* should take priority (Hayden et al. 2003).

!

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
A diagrammatic representation of the lifecycle of *Ulva* (a green bladed alga).

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
(Adapted from Dawson 1956)

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
The life cycle of *Ulva* is fairly representative of many green algae.

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
In *Ulva,* there is an alternation between the bladed sporophyte or the asexual plant and the gametophytes (female and male plants).

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
The four flagellated spores (zoospores) germinate, developing into the gametophyte plants.

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
The gametophytes in turn produce flagellate gametes (the male gametes being smaller than the female).

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
The fusion between the male and female gametes results in the zygote, which develops into the sporophyte plant.

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
This life history is known as *isomorphic alternation of generations*.

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
#### *Rhodophyta* **(red algae)**

The majority of red algae are said to have three generations, unlike the two (sporophyte and gametophyte) seen in the Chlorophyta.

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
In red algae, there is the sporophyte (tetrasporophyte) generation, the gametophyte generation and the carposporophyte generation, the latter remains attached (or parasitic) to the female gametophyte.

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
The carposporophyte releases its carpospores which then develop into tetrasporophytes and the life-history continues.

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
A simplified diagram of the red algal life-history is provided (Figure 3) using *Polysiphonia*, a genus comprising of mostly small epiphytic plants.

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
The tetrasporophyte plant (first generation) produces four sporangia, which develop into equal number of male and female gametophytes (second generation).

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
The male plant releases his gametes (spermatia), which are non-motile.

--- SENTENCE 119 ---
The spermatia fuse with the female sex organ (carpogonium) on the female plant.

--- SENTENCE 120 ---
Once fertilization takes place the third generation or the carposporophyte begins development on or in the female plant.

--- SENTENCE 121 ---
The releasing of the carpospores and their development begins the sporophytic phase and the alternation of generations is repeated.

!

--- SENTENCE 122 ---
[](_page_8_Picture_5.jpeg)

Figure 3. *Polysiphonia* life-cycle (adapted from South & Whittick 1987)

#### *Phaeophyta* **(brown algae)**

Brown algae have many variations in its life-history, including a isomorphic lifehistory, that is the gametophyte and the sporophyte generations are identical in their morphological appearance.

--- SENTENCE 123 ---
It can also have a heteromorphic life-history where the gametophyte generation looks different from the sporophyte generation.

--- SENTENCE 124 ---
For example the gametophyte may take the form of minute filaments or prostrate expanding crust about a few centimeters long, whereas the sporophyte could be a large, fleshy, bladed alga that grows to 10m or more tall.

--- SENTENCE 125 ---
Most of the brown algae from American Samoa appear to have isomorphic life-histories, although culture studies of the species need to be carried out to verify this.

!

--- SENTENCE 126 ---
A diagrammatic representation of the life-cycle of *Hincksia*, a genus recorded from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 127 ---
The sporophyte releases the zoospores from the unilocular sporangium, which germinate and begin the gametophyte generation.

--- SENTENCE 128 ---
The gametophytes produce gametes in the plurilocular sporangia, which then fuse to form the sporophyte generation.

--- SENTENCE 129 ---
If the male gametes fail to find female gametes then they can develop directly to form male gametophyte plants, likewise with female gametes.

--- SENTENCE 130 ---
#### *Algae – diversity in forms*

As stated earlier, algae are plants at their most simple form.

--- SENTENCE 131 ---
The algal body is referred to as the thallus (plural – thalli).

--- SENTENCE 132 ---
The thallus can be filamentous, bladed, unicellular, multicellular, siphonous, crust-like, fan-like, umbrella-like, vesicular, hollow, articulated, non-articulated, to name a few.

--- SENTENCE 133 ---
The texture can be membranous, cartilaginous, rock-like, lubricious, glabrous, tough, firm, soft, delicate, fragile, prickly, calcified, coriaceous, etc.

--- SENTENCE 134 ---
The color generally is indicative of the group it belongs to, but this is not always the case.

--- SENTENCE 135 ---
For example, a red alga often belongs to the Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 136 ---
However, there are many Rhodophyta which are yellow, orange, dark green, black or blue in color.

--- SENTENCE 137 ---
The same can be said of the Phaeophyta, Chlorophyta and Cyanophyta.

--- SENTENCE 138 ---
Algae are attached by various means.

--- SENTENCE 139 ---
The term for root is holdfast, and there can be many designs of holdfasts.

--- SENTENCE 140 ---
In some algae, only one holdfast is known (eg. *Sargassum anapense*) others have many (e.g. *Halimeda opuntia*).

--- SENTENCE 141 ---
The branching pattern of some algae is very important in determining not only species but genera Figure 5 highlights some of the different branching forms that can be found in algae.

!

--- SENTENCE 142 ---
a. unbranched or simple.

--- SENTENCE 143 ---
e. secund or pectinate.

--- SENTENCE 144 ---
#### *Algal classification*

Algae straddle two kingdoms (Plantae and Monera), but this is debatable and it depends largely on whose classification one chooses to follow.

--- SENTENCE 145 ---
Nevertheless, the fact that two high levels of classification are included is remarkable.

--- SENTENCE 146 ---
What this means is that algae are polyphyletic, or have evolved from several very different ancestors.

--- SENTENCE 147 ---
The kingdom Plantae incorporates all of the terrestrial plants, seagrasses and the Rhodophyta, Chlorophyta and Phaeophyta.

--- SENTENCE 148 ---
They are autotrophic, photosynthetic plants that may be single-celled (unicellular) or composed of many cells (multicellular).

--- SENTENCE 149 ---
In the marine environment, algae are the dominant groups, however, other plants such as seagrasses and mangroves provide a high biomass and important habitats for marine and terrestrial organisms.

--- SENTENCE 150 ---
The Kingdom Monera includes the Cyanophyta (blue-green algae) and Schizomycophyta (true bacteria).

--- SENTENCE 151 ---
It is characterized by prokaryotic organisms that lack most of the organs found in the kingdom Plantae.

--- SENTENCE 152 ---
The blue-green algae that are frequently encountered comprised a small component of Cyanophyta, which varies from a single cell to simple colonies, to extensive pseudo-branched filaments.

--- SENTENCE 153 ---
Approximately 98% of red algae are found in the sea.

--- SENTENCE 154 ---
The Phaeophyta has about 99% of its species that are marine.

--- SENTENCE 155 ---
In contrast to the Chlorophyta has only 10% of its species found in the marine environment (Dawes 1981).

--- SENTENCE 156 ---
The red algae are most common in tropical to subtropical waters, whereas the brown algae dominate cooler, more temperate waters.

--- SENTENCE 157 ---
Because seaweeds and seagrasses need light for photosynthesis, most of the marine plants are found in the upper edge of the oceans.

--- SENTENCE 158 ---
The deepest depth where a seaweed was collected by means of dredging is 295 m (Littler, M., et al. 1985), but most are found in the inter tidal to shallow subtidal places.

#### **Seagrasses**

Seagrasses are flowering plants belonging to the phylum Magnoliophyta (kingdom Plantae), and the class Angiospermae.

--- SENTENCE 159 ---
They are a specialized group of plants that have adapted to living in aquatic environments.

--- SENTENCE 160 ---
About 50 seagrass species are recorded throughout the world, with the highest diversity found in the central Indo-Pacific region.

--- SENTENCE 161 ---
As with most marine animals and plants, the species diversity peters out as one moves away from this centre of the diversity.

--- SENTENCE 162 ---
In tropical parts of Australia, 14 seagrass species are known, decreasing to five in Fiji, three in Western Samoa and none in the Cook Islands.

--- SENTENCE 163 ---
Seagrasses have rhizomes or horizontal running stems that enable them to grow.

--- SENTENCE 164 ---
## *1.3 Algae and coral reefs*

Algae play a vital role in coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 165 ---
Algae and seagrasses are the primary producers of the sea.

--- SENTENCE 166 ---
They are autotrophic or self-feeding, manufacturing their own food through chlorophyll and the photosynthesis process.

--- SENTENCE 167 ---
Some algae and seagrasses form large meadows providing unique niches for economically important fishes, shellfish and other invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
Some plants act as filters and stabilizers, binding sediments, thus lessening sediment impact on corals and other organisms on the reef.

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
A meadow of seagrasses or algae can absorb wave energy, thus reducing the potential negative impacts of wave surge along vulnerable coasts.

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
Another important role of algae is more evident when they die, and their skeletal remains form an important sandy substratum in some reefs.

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
The atolls provide a good example of this, with the green calcified *Halimeda* species forming a major component of the atoll substratum.

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
Below the surging waves near the reef crest and the spur and groove zones, attractive fleshy algal species, which may be found in the open or more often in cryptic places, provide a kaleidoscopic scene complemented by the color of the crustose corallines.

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
This forms a lasting impression on divers and visitors of the underwater world, boosting tourism and providing revenues for the local economy.

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
In the inter tidal zones and tide-pools, algae are often the dominant life form seen.

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
Their ubiquitous nature means they can withstand the pounding waves on the reef crest, the incessant fluctuation of sea-flow and high water temperatures, and in some cases the lack of seawater for hours during hot tropical days.

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
This variation of environmental conditions gives rise to species associated with specific habitats, such as the mangrove algal community, referred to as the 'bostrychietum' (the name was coined by E. Post, a phycologist, in reference to the dominant presence of the algal genus *Bostrychia*).

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
The mangrove algal community of American Samoa is yet to be properly surveyed, whereas that of the neighboring Western Samoa (Upolu) was recently surveyed by Skelton & South (2002).

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
Persistent and consistent observations and monitoring over time would often reveal a familiar trend whereby reef conditions when once restored would have a phase-shift from turf-algal community, superseded by crustose coralline algae, which effectively provide substrata for planulae to settle and grow.

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
The coralline algae continue to play an important role during most phases of reef recovery, by consolidating loose carbonate structure and thus providing a stabilized reef.

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
They also provide food for specialized feeders such as parrotfish (Scaridae).

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
Seagrasses role in the coral reefs are many.

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
Six functions are listed below:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
They stabilize and hold bottom sediments even through the enormous stresses of hurricanes and storms.

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
The leaves retard water currents and waves, promoting sedimentation of particulate matter and inhibiting re-suspension of organic and inorganic matter.
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 185 ---
The meadow serves as a shelter and refuge for resident and transient adult and juvenile animals, many of which are of commercial and recreational importance.

--- SENTENCE 186 ---
The feeding pathways consist of both direct grazing on the leaves or epiphytes and detrital pathways.

--- SENTENCE 187 ---
The plants attain a high production and growth (leaves of some species can grow 5-10mm per day).
- 6.

--- SENTENCE 188 ---
The plants produce and trap detritus and secrete dissolved organic matter that tends to internalize nutrient cycles with the ecosystem

### *1.4 Samoans and seaweeds*

!

--- SENTENCE 189 ---
Captions: (T) Boy selling seaweeds*.*

--- SENTENCE 190 ---
(L) An *ofu* (bundle) of seaweed (*Caulerpa racemosa*)

--- SENTENCE 191 ---
*Limu* is the generic Samoan term used for seagrasses, mosses and freshwater weeds.

--- SENTENCE 192 ---
The traditional knowledge of seaweeds in Samoa is limited to species that are used either for food or for medicinal purposes.

--- SENTENCE 193 ---
Twenty seaweeds and two seagrass names are known.

--- SENTENCE 194 ---
Other seaweeds are simply referred to as *limu*.

--- SENTENCE 195 ---
The list of Samoan seaweed and seagrass names given below is compiled from George Pratt (1876) and from the author's understanding of currently used seaweed names.

--- SENTENCE 196 ---
*Limu a'a* – the name (a'a) literally means the fibers of a root, which may refer to the rhizoidal system of *Sargassum* or *Turbinaria* species.

--- SENTENCE 197 ---
*Limu a'au* – reef seaweed.

--- SENTENCE 198 ---
Refers to *Halymenia durvillei*, a seaweed that is often collected near the reef.

--- SENTENCE 199 ---
*Limu a laea* – the name probably refers to a seaweed that is eaten by the *laea* (parrotfish - Scaridae).

--- SENTENCE 200 ---
This could be a coralline alga.

--- SENTENCE 201 ---
*Limu 'ava* – the name 'ava may refer to the beardlike shape of this seaweed.

--- SENTENCE 202 ---
According to Pratt (1876)

--- SENTENCE 203 ---
this is an edible seaweed, which may be synonymous with limu a'au or limu mumu (*Halymenia durvillei*).

--- SENTENCE 204 ---
*Limu 'ula* – there are two probable meanings of 'ula.

--- SENTENCE 205 ---
The first refers to a necklace and the second to the color red.

--- SENTENCE 206 ---
Pratt (1876) refers to it as an edible seaweed, which again may be synonymous with limu a'au (*Halymenia durvillei*).

--- SENTENCE 207 ---
*Limu faaleaga-mea* – destructive weed, referring to *Sargassum* spp., and the way they take over the

shallow reef, depriving corals from growing.

--- SENTENCE 208 ---
*Limu foe* – paddle weed (*Halophila ovalis* and *Halophila minor*).

--- SENTENCE 209 ---
*Limu fuafua* – the word literally means an abscess (pimple) of the skin, in reference to the bubble shape of this edible seaweed (*Caulerpa racemosa*)

*Limu lau-ago* – this seaweed refers to the leaf of the Ago plant, a tumeric plant (*Curcuma longa*) that is widely used by the Samoans for medicinal purposes.

--- SENTENCE 210 ---
The leaves of this tumeric plant resemble *Halophila ovalis* or *H. minor* (paddle weed).

--- SENTENCE 211 ---
*Limu laumei* – turtle weed (*Chlorodesmis fastigiata*)

--- SENTENCE 212 ---
*Limu lautaliga* – ear-like seaweed (*Padina boryana*)

--- SENTENCE 213 ---
*Limu limu* – a detached seaweed; probably referring to the free floating green alga *Cladophoropsis.*

--- SENTENCE 214 ---
*Limu mumu* – red seaweed.

--- SENTENCE 215 ---
Although there are red seaweeds, this particular use refers to *Halymenia durvillei*.

--- SENTENCE 216 ---
*Limu pata* – although there are many meanings of 'pata', perhaps the most appropriate use of this word from a seaweed perspective is 'blister'.

--- SENTENCE 217 ---
It may refer to a seaweed (probably a cyanophyte) that causes blister when one comes into contact with it.

--- SENTENCE 218 ---
*Lim usu* – this may refer to a waxy, watery type of seaweed that has a similar substance found in taro or breadfruit.

--- SENTENCE 219 ---
*Limu taemoa* – the word 'taemoa' refers to chicken manure; the name may be in reference to a Cyanophyta (*Phormidium* sp.)

--- SENTENCE 220 ---
that may look like chicken manure; or alternatively it may refer to the smell caused by *Trichodesmium* bloom (red tides).

--- SENTENCE 221 ---
*Limu tala* – prickly or thorny seaweed, perhaps referring to the tough rhizoidal system of *Sargassum* spp.

--- SENTENCE 222 ---
and *Turbinaria ornata* that may have caught the feet of fishers.

--- SENTENCE 223 ---
Another name that has been used is *Limu falaleagamea*, meaning destructive weed.

--- SENTENCE 224 ---
*Limu to'o* – 'to'o refers to a pole that was used to propel the canoe forward.

--- SENTENCE 225 ---
It is unclear what this seaweed is, but it may be attributed to seaweeds growing on the pole, which include the green alga – *Enteromorpha intestinalis.*

--- SENTENCE 226 ---
*Limu vai* – a freshwater weed.

--- SENTENCE 227 ---
*Limu vao* – a grass weed, referring to the morphology of *Syringodium isoetifolium*.

--- SENTENCE 228 ---
In attempting to revitalize this endangered knowledge, new names are devised for some of the commonly encountered seaweeds.

--- SENTENCE 229 ---
As this is a working document, additional Samoan names will be added once consultation has been carried out with relevant organizations and individuals.

--- SENTENCE 230 ---
## *1.5 Historical aspects of algal research in American Samoa*

American Samoa's algal flora was first assessed by William Albert Setchell in the early 1920's (Setchell 1924); 100 species was compiled.

--- SENTENCE 231 ---
Setchell's collections were limited to shallow inter tidal areas, with a few subtidal specimens obtained by dredging.

--- SENTENCE 232 ---
No other major algal work is known from American Samoa since Setchell, but the efforts of some sporadic and itinerant algal collectors passing through the islands have yielded some new additions to the island's flora.

--- SENTENCE 233 ---
The recent publication of the South Pacific Reef Plants by Diane and Mark Littler (2003) illustrates 33 algal species from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 234 ---
The Littlers' algal collections are housed at the Smithsonian Institution, Washington.

--- SENTENCE 235 ---
Other notable phycologists to visit American Samoa include Isabella Abbott, Roy Tsuda, Paul Gabrielson and Peter Vroom; the latter two collectors' collections are yet to be curated.

--- SENTENCE 236 ---
American Samoan algae are also noted from reef ecologists' reports (Dahl 1971;

--- SENTENCE 237 ---
Birkeland *et al.* 1987; Hunter *et al.* 1993; Wilkins in Birkeland *et al.* 1995).

--- SENTENCE 238 ---
Moreover, American Samoa algae were included in the algal checklist of the Samoan Archipelago by Skelton & South (1999), where 198 taxa were listed.

--- SENTENCE 239 ---
The checklist is currently being updated as past collections housed at the Bishop Museum and the University of California at Berkley Herbarium are examined; together with continuing field surveys, adding new records as well as new species to the flora.

--- SENTENCE 240 ---
A total of 318 species have so far been documented (Skelton unpubl.

--- SENTENCE 241 ---
data) for the Archipelago.

## **2.0 MATERIALS AND METHODS**

The coordinates for the designated core sites (Table 1) were entered on an *E-Trex* Global Positioning System.

--- SENTENCE 251 ---
Most macroalgae seen during the surveys were noted, some were photographed and collected.

--- SENTENCE 252 ---
All specimens were soaked in a 5% formalin/seawater solution for a minimum of 2 days.

--- SENTENCE 253 ---
The solution was then decanted and recycled.

--- SENTENCE 254 ---
Specimens were pressed using a standard plant press during the surveys were given to DMWR as reference material.

--- SENTENCE 255 ---
Other specimens were analyzed in Australia, with the aid of microscopes (Nikon SMZ645 dissecting microscope and an Olympus CX31 compound microscope).

--- SENTENCE 256 ---
Underwater photos of algae and habitats were taken with a Sanyo AZ3 digital camera.

--- SENTENCE 257 ---
Microscopic photos and habit shots of algae above water were taken using a Nikon Coolpix 990 digital camera.

--- SENTENCE 258 ---
The images were processed using Photoshop 6 software.

--- SENTENCE 259 ---
All processed specimens were recorded in a log book and the data were later entered into a Microsoft Excel program.

--- SENTENCE 260 ---
Pressed specimens are housed at Bishop Museum (BISH) and the South Pacific Regional Herbarium (SUVA-A).

## **3.0 RESULTS**

### *3.1

--- SENTENCE 290 ---
An Illustrated guide to common and interesting macro-algae of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 291 ---
*

Of the 239 species recorded from the surveys, most of the algae were small (less than 5 cm tall).

--- SENTENCE 292 ---
In this section, 67 species are described and illustrated.

--- SENTENCE 293 ---
Some of the illustrated species are small but are included to enable the reader to appreciate the diversity of these marine organisms.

--- SENTENCE 294 ---
This section is arranged alphabetically within the five divisions or phyla that were surveyed, beginning with the Rhodophyta (red seaweeds), Phaeophyta (brown seaweeds), Chlorophyta (green seaweeds), Cyanophyta (blue-green seaweeds) and the Magnoliophyta (seagrasses).

--- SENTENCE 295 ---
The technical terms are kept to a minimum, but in some cases, these cannot be avoided.

--- SENTENCE 296 ---
Hence, a glossary is provided to explain these terms.

--- SENTENCE 297 ---
The descriptions are based on personal observations and on published accounts.

--- SENTENCE 298 ---
The species name (scientific name) is provided, followed by the author (the person or persons that described the species).

--- SENTENCE 299 ---
This is then followed by the English name or Common Name (abbreviated CN:) where known.

--- SENTENCE 300 ---
The Samoan Name (abbreviated SN:) is also recorded.

--- SENTENCE 301 ---
The asterisk (\*) denotes a new name for the alga, suggested by the author.

#### **RHODOPHYTA (red algae, red seaweeds)**

*Actinotrichia fragilis* (Forsskål) Børgesen (CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 302 ---
SN: \*Limu felefele = bushy seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 303 ---
A common seaweed that is found from reef flats to deeper waters.

--- SENTENCE 304 ---
It can form large clumps on rubble, rocks or at the base of corals.

--- SENTENCE 305 ---
It is also found attached to other algae, especially *Halimeda* species.

--- SENTENCE 306 ---
The color is variable from bright red, orange, yellow-brown to white, and the plants can be tightly clumped or loose in appearance.

--- SENTENCE 307 ---
It is fairly wiry and can easily be identified by the small hair-like filaments that form small bands around the thallus.

--- SENTENCE 308 ---
The branching resembles a Y (dichotomous) and it can be wide or narrow angled.

--- SENTENCE 309 ---
This seaweed has calcium embedded within the cell walls.

--- SENTENCE 310 ---
**Remarks:** *Actinotrichia fragilis* is the only confirmed species of this genus reported

#### *Amphiroa foliacea* Lamouroux

(CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 311 ---
SN: \*Limu talatala = prickly/rough seaweed).

!

--- SENTENCE 312 ---
This branched and calcified seaweed is common from shallow reef flats to deeper waters.

--- SENTENCE 313 ---
The color is variable from pink, white to dark red.

--- SENTENCE 314 ---
The seaweed is delicate, breaking when handled, but some are fairly rigid.

--- SENTENCE 315 ---
It can form large clumps.

--- SENTENCE 316 ---
The branching is dichotomous, but adventitious branches may arise in any part of the plant.

--- SENTENCE 317 ---
A midrib can be seen in some thalli.

--- SENTENCE 318 ---
The surface is smooth, or appears lumpy when it is reproductive.

--- SENTENCE 319 ---
The alga grows to 10cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 320 ---
Four species of this genus are known from American Samoa, but this is

the most common.

--- SENTENCE 321 ---
*Asparagopsis taxiformis* (Delile) Trevisan

(CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 322 ---
SN: \*Limu vavai = soft and flexible seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 323 ---
In tropical to subtropical waters.

**Description:**

--- SENTENCE 324 ---
This seaweed is soft and delicate with the outer fine branches easily broken off if not carefully handled.

--- SENTENCE 325 ---
The color is variable from bright pink, to pale red to grayish.

--- SENTENCE 326 ---
The lower part of the thallus lacks branches.

--- SENTENCE 327 ---
The plant is attached by firm creeping rhizoids.

--- SENTENCE 328 ---
This interesting seaweed has two growth forms.

--- SENTENCE 329 ---
The male and female form looks like that shown in the picture, and can reach 10cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 330 ---
The tetrasporophyte form looks like fine hairs, and is often found in turf communities and is only a few millimeters tall.

--- SENTENCE 331 ---
The seaweed is eaten in Hawaii and Indonesia.

--- SENTENCE 332 ---
In the Philippines this seaweed is used for medicine (Zemke-White and Ohno *Bostrychia tenella* (Lamouroux) J. Agardh (CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 333 ---
SN: \*Limu togo = mangrove seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 334 ---
This small seaweed is often associated with the mangrove forest.

--- SENTENCE 335 ---
Because mangrove forests in American Samoa are small and declining, this seaweed is better seen forming large hanging fuzz above seawater sprayed areas.

--- SENTENCE 336 ---
The color varies from dark red, to orange, yellow and brown.

--- SENTENCE 337 ---
The photo above shows part of the extensive fuzz made by this seaweed.

--- SENTENCE 338 ---
An individual thallus is, however, very small growing to 3cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 339 ---
The thallus is attached to the substratum by a holdfast made up of many downward growing cells.

--- SENTENCE 340 ---
The reproductive structures are borne on modified branches called stichidia (singular = stichidium).

--- SENTENCE 341 ---
As shown in the photo left, the tetraspores (dark round balls) are housed inside the stichidium.

--- SENTENCE 342 ---
**Remarks:** *Bostrychia tenella* is a special seaweed that has adapted well to growing in a harsh

environment.

--- SENTENCE 343 ---
It receives sea-spray during high tides, and is exposed to the heat of the sun during low tides.

--- SENTENCE 344 ---
The salinity from the water spray can be fairly salty during hot times, whereas it can be very low during wet seasons.

--- SENTENCE 345 ---
Tropical to subtropical regions.

#### *Botryocladia skottsbergii* (Børgesen) Levring

CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 346 ---
SN: \*Limu fuamanu = birds egg seaweed.

!

--- SENTENCE 347 ---
This beautiful seaweed is cryptic, growing in crevices or on the underside of large boulders and volcanic rocks in exposed places.

--- SENTENCE 348 ---
The plants are red, maroon to orange color and grow to 4cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 349 ---
The plant is attached by a disc holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 350 ---
The seaweed resembles small grapes, growing solitary or in groups (as seen in the photo).

--- SENTENCE 351 ---
The stolon is tough, bearing the grape-like vesicles.

--- SENTENCE 352 ---
The vesicles are filled with a

mucilaginous substance.

--- SENTENCE 353 ---
This alga was collected from heavily exposed places.

--- SENTENCE 354 ---
Littler and Littler (2003) recorded *Botryocladia tenuissima* Taylor from American Samoa (not found in this survey), a Caribbean species not yet known from the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 355 ---
Their illustration and description is sufficient to clearly separate it from *B. skottsbergii.

--- SENTENCE 356 ---
Tropical to subtropical Waters

#### *Ceramium flaccidum* (Kützing)

--- SENTENCE 357 ---
Ardissone

(CM: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 358 ---
\*Limu mumulaititi = small red seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 359 ---
This epiphytic seaweed is very common, often forming red fuzz on larger seaweeds.

--- SENTENCE 360 ---
It grows to about 0.5cm tall in tropical waters and up to 10cm in cooler waters (Womersley 1998).

--- SENTENCE 361 ---
The use of a hand-lens or a microscope is necessary to differentiate this species from about ten other *Ceramium* species known from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 362 ---
It has a square shaped node and internode system.

--- SENTENCE 363 ---
The nodes are composed of small cells.

--- SENTENCE 364 ---
The most distinctive feature of this alga is the transverse or elongated basal-most cells.

--- SENTENCE 365 ---
The alga has a prostrate system, which looks like the erect thallus as seen in the photo.

--- SENTENCE 366 ---
Rhizoids are produced from the prostrate system terminating in finger-like tips.

--- SENTENCE 367 ---
In some specimens, some of the cells appear very dark when stained (gland cells).

--- SENTENCE 368 ---
Other specimens have colorless

hair that easily falls off, whereas others lack the hairs or gland cells.

--- SENTENCE 369 ---
This alga is one of the few algae that are near cosmopolitan.

--- SENTENCE 370 ---
It is found in cold waters in South Australia and Europe as well as throughout all tropical regions.

--- SENTENCE 371 ---
Tropical to subtropical and temperate seas.

--- SENTENCE 372 ---
(Decaisne) Piccone

(CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 373 ---
SN: \*Limu fele-laititi = small club seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 374 ---
A delicate seaweed that is brittle and grows in shade provided by larger seaweeds.

--- SENTENCE 375 ---
It is heavily calcified, pinkish to light red color, and grows to 3 cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 376 ---
It often forms clumps with other turf algae along the front reef or on exposed places.

--- SENTENCE 377 ---
The plant is composed of segments in a series, with each segment loosely resembling a club as seen in playing cards (tri-lobed).

--- SENTENCE 378 ---
The side lobes are rounded tapering to a sharp point, whereas the middle top lobe bears the next segment.

--- SENTENCE 379 ---
A non-calcified region separates one segment from the next, thus giving the alga a limited flexibility.

--- SENTENCE 380 ---
The lower part of the alga is cylindrical, resembling closely

arranged beads on a string.

--- SENTENCE 381 ---
This section lacks branching, with the branches confined largely to the mid to top part of the plant.

--- SENTENCE 382 ---
The branches are flat and are arranged in one plane.

--- SENTENCE 383 ---
A rhizoidal holdfast system is produced near the base, which when in contact with the substratum forms disc-pads.

--- SENTENCE 384 ---
The reproductive structures are born on the two side lobes.

--- SENTENCE 385 ---
The fine segments and the smaller size of this species distinguish it from the other two species known from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 386 ---
**Distribution:** Tropical Indo-Pacific.

!

--- SENTENCE 387 ---
**A field habitat shot of** *Cheilosporum acutilobum* **from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 388 ---
Photo: from Littler and Littler 2003.

--- SENTENCE 389 ---
** 

*Cheilosporum spectabile* forma *elegans* (possibly a new form)

--- SENTENCE 390 ---
SN: \*Limu fele-mosemose = beautiful spearhead seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 391 ---
This elegant seaweed grows in cryptic places, especially in crevices and on sheltered sides of large boulders and overhangs.

--- SENTENCE 392 ---
It is difficult to collect as it often wedges itself between boulders.

--- SENTENCE 393 ---
It grows down to 10 m deep and has a maroon to pinkish color.

--- SENTENCE 394 ---
Branching is dichotomous, albeit sparingly.

--- SENTENCE 395 ---
It tends to hang downwards, rather than having an erect form as seen in the other *Cheilosporum*  species.

--- SENTENCE 396 ---
It grows to 10 cm tall, and often in small clumps.

--- SENTENCE 397 ---
The structure is similar to the other *Cheilosporum* species, with a tri-lobed segment arrangement.

--- SENTENCE 398 ---
It is delicate, although more flexible than the other species.

--- SENTENCE 399 ---
This is possibly a new form of *Cheilosporum spectabile*, although more studies of its reproductive and vegetative structures are needed to ascertain this.

--- SENTENCE 400 ---
**Distribution**: Samoa (so far known only from American Samoa).

--- SENTENCE 401 ---
#### *Cheilosporum spectabile* Harvey ex Grunow

(CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 402 ---
SN: \*Limu ulutao-lapo'a = large spearhead seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 403 ---
This common alga is found mainly in subtidal places (20m depth), but is also found in exposed shallow places.

--- SENTENCE 404 ---
The color is pink, although various shades of red and white are also seen.

--- SENTENCE 405 ---
The size varies from 3- 10cm tall, forming bushy clumps.

--- SENTENCE 406 ---
It is firmly attached by discoid holdfast to the substratum, preferring carbonate structures.

--- SENTENCE 407 ---
The tri-lobed segments are firm and can be caught up with other segments enabling it to withstand strong wave actions.

--- SENTENCE 408 ---
The tip of the segments is usually rounded.

--- SENTENCE 409 ---
The branching is more common from the mid to the upper parts,

and the segments are flattened and in one plane.

--- SENTENCE 410 ---
Although dichotomous branching is common, adventitious branches may give an otherwise impression.

--- SENTENCE 411 ---
The reproductive structures are found on upper portions of the side tri-lobed segments.

--- SENTENCE 412 ---
This is by far the most common *Cheilosporum* species from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 413 ---
[](_page_23_Picture_7.jpeg)

A dried *Cheilosporum spectabile* specimen.

--- SENTENCE 414 ---
Photo: Skelton & IOI (Australia).

--- SENTENCE 415 ---
Grunow

CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 416 ---
(Photo from Littler and Littler 2003)

!

--- SENTENCE 417 ---
This fleshy alga is found in crevices and in sheltered places in usually turbulent environments.

--- SENTENCE 418 ---
It grows close to the substratum and has a deep red color, similar to a crustose coralline alga, thus it can be hard to detect.

--- SENTENCE 419 ---
The alga is firm, smooth and with many lobes.

--- SENTENCE 420 ---
It is attached at various points by holdfasts that come into contact with the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 421 ---
The inside of the alga is filled with a thick mucilaginous gel.

--- SENTENCE 422 ---
The plants spread to 10cm wide.

--- SENTENCE 423 ---
Anastomozing branches are common in this alga.

--- SENTENCE 424 ---
This alga is rarely collected, and it takes a trained eye and a lot of luck in spotting it.

--- SENTENCE 425 ---
It may have been overlooked due to its close resemblance of various crustose coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 426 ---
Indo-Pacific, with the type locality from Papua New Guinea.

--- SENTENCE 427 ---
#### *Corynocystis prostrata* Kraft

CN: Not Known.

--- SENTENCE 428 ---
**Description:** Plant color varies from olive green to red.

--- SENTENCE 429 ---
The prostrate blades are flat to 10mm wide and to 15cm long.

--- SENTENCE 430 ---
It is attached by many small cylindrical peg-like roots.

--- SENTENCE 431 ---
The club-shaped blades tend to be rounded or have a slight dip on the apices.

--- SENTENCE 432 ---
The branches are produced from the blade margin and are basally constricted.

--- SENTENCE 433 ---
New blades can develop from parts where grazers grazed.

--- SENTENCE 434 ---
Anastomozing amongst the blades is very common.

--- SENTENCE 435 ---
This alga may have been misidentified as *Prionitis formosana*  (a species found from American Samoa).

--- SENTENCE 436 ---
Chou

CN: Not know.

--- SENTENCE 437 ---
SN: \*Limu fulufulua = hairy seaweed.

!

--- SENTENCE 438 ---
The alga is often solitary growing in many different habitats, from silted places to healthy coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 439 ---
It tends to grow in shaded places and usually on carbonate structures.

--- SENTENCE 440 ---
The color is dark brown to maroon and quite bushy in appearance.

--- SENTENCE 441 ---
It grows to 8cm tall and is attached by a disclike holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 442 ---
The branches are dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 443 ---
It has a distinctive furry and soft feel to the touch.

--- SENTENCE 444 ---
This is one of four *Galaxaura* species recorded from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 445 ---
It is distinguished from the others by its hairy filaments and 'dirty' appearance.

--- SENTENCE 446 ---
*Galaxaura marginata* (Ellis & Solander) Lamouroux

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 447 ---
This alga is variable in color, from light green to white, and red.

--- SENTENCE 448 ---
The thallus is compressed to flattened and glabrous, lacking any hairs throughout, except near the cylindrical holdfast, where minute hairs can be seen.

--- SENTENCE 449 ---
It can be solitary or form large clumps, with individual plants to 10cm tall or more.

--- SENTENCE 450 ---
It is often collected from deeper waters (5-10m), usually in sheltered places.

--- SENTENCE 451 ---
The branches are dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 452 ---
The axes are broad about 5mm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 453 ---
A concentric pattern can be seen in

the thallus of some plants, especially near the top.

--- SENTENCE 454 ---
The tips taper slightly but are never pointed.

--- SENTENCE 455 ---
This is perhaps the most distinctive of all *Galaxaura* species by its flattened thallus that is devoid of hairs, except inconspicuous hairs near the cylindrical holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 456 ---
*Galaxaura obtusata* (found from Samoa), also has a smooth thallus but is larger and cylindrical.

--- SENTENCE 457 ---
*Gelidiella acerosa* (Forsskål) Feldmann & Hamel CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 458 ---
SN: \*Limu uaea = wire-like seaweed.

!

--- SENTENCE 459 ---
This alga is very tough, wiry, and cartilaginous to 10cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 460 ---
It grows in basaltic rock in very exposed places.

--- SENTENCE 461 ---
The color is purple, dark red to black.

--- SENTENCE 462 ---
The plant often grows in an arcuate or arched manner bearing erect branches.

--- SENTENCE 463 ---
The branching is sparse, alternate or opposite.

--- SENTENCE 464 ---
The basal part of the plant creeps along the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 465 ---
The thallus is cylindrical or slightly compressed, and is attached by a tough rhizoidal system.

--- SENTENCE 466 ---
Plying them off the rocks is often difficult, especially if one has to watch for the waves.

--- SENTENCE 467 ---
This alga is harvested for its agar property in India, Malaysia and Vietnam, whereas it is edible in the Philippines.

--- SENTENCE 468 ---
Tropical to subtropical places.

--- SENTENCE 469 ---
*Gelidiopsis repens* (Kützing) Weber-van Bosse

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 470 ---
The alga forms short but very busy communities that are very firm and compact.

--- SENTENCE 471 ---
It grows in most habitats, from the shallow inter tidal to 25m depth.

--- SENTENCE 472 ---
The thallus is firm and has a terete basal system, giving rise to erect blades of up to 5-8cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 473 ---
Two to five dichotomous branches can be closely arranged, giving the impression of a palm.

--- SENTENCE 474 ---
The branches are 0.5-4mm wide, with the mid-section being the broadest and the tip the narrowest.

--- SENTENCE 475 ---
This alga is an important member of the turf algal community.

--- SENTENCE 476 ---
Tropical waters.

#### *Gelidiopsis intricata* (C. Agardh) Vickers

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 477 ---
This alga is variable in color, from light green to red.

--- SENTENCE 478 ---
It is wiry, terete or sub-terete and can grow to 10cm tall, from inter tidal to sub tidal places.

--- SENTENCE 479 ---
It forms large clumps.

--- SENTENCE 480 ---
The branching is uncommon and the cylindrical thallus tapers towards the tip.

--- SENTENCE 481 ---
It is attached by a wiry often entangled rhizoidal system.

--- SENTENCE 482 ---
This alga may be confused with another red alga, *Coelothrix irregularis*.

--- SENTENCE 483 ---
The two can be separated by the

iridescent and hollow middle of the latter alga.

--- SENTENCE 484 ---
This alga is an important member of the turf algal community in subtidal areas.

--- SENTENCE 485 ---
**Distribution:** Tropical to temperate seas.

--- SENTENCE 486 ---
#### *Haloplegma duperreyi* Montagne

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 487 ---
SN: \*Limu masaesae = torn seaweed)

!

--- SENTENCE 488 ---
The appearance of this alga is that of a red piece of paper that has been rubbed over a rough surface.

--- SENTENCE 489 ---
In reality this is one of the most beautiful algae when viewed with a dissecting microscope or hand-lens.

--- SENTENCE 490 ---
It is red to maroon in color and grow to 5cm broad.

--- SENTENCE 491 ---
It is found in shaded places, on large boulders or bommies in shallow subtidal places (2-5m depth).

--- SENTENCE 492 ---
The soft blade alga, is composed of an intricate network of monosiphonous filaments, forming up to 5 layers.

#### *Halymenia durvillei* Bory de Saint Vincent CN: Durville's alga.

--- SENTENCE 493 ---
SN: Limu aau, limu mumu, limu 'ava, limu 'ula

!

--- SENTENCE 494 ---
[](_page_28_Picture_2.jpeg)

**Distribution:** Tropical to subtropical seas.

--- SENTENCE 495 ---
This is one of the most beautiful large algae common in Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 496 ---
The plants are delicate and the color varies from orange, red, and maroon, brown to light pink.

--- SENTENCE 497 ---
The thallus is either delicate and soft or bladed and firm, growing to 1m tall.

--- SENTENCE 498 ---
It grows in shallow places, like near river mouth and inter tidal zone, or in subtidal to 20m deep.

--- SENTENCE 499 ---
It is attached by a discoid holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 500 ---
The branching is bushy, irregular with the blades having small teeth (dentations).

--- SENTENCE 501 ---
Known by various Samoan names, this alga is eaten by mixing it with coconut cream and baking it in the traditional oven (*umu*).

--- SENTENCE 502 ---
It also has good potential for bioprospecting, having anti lung cancer compounds.

--- SENTENCE 503 ---
*Hydrolithon onkodes* (Heydrich) Penrose & Woelkerling CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 504 ---
SN: \*Limu ma'a or ?

--- SENTENCE 505 ---
Limu a laea

!

--- SENTENCE 506 ---
This common coralline alga grows in wave exposed places from the inter tidal to subtidal.

--- SENTENCE 507 ---
Its shape and color are variable, but is usually light pink in well lit area to dark red in shaded places.

--- SENTENCE 508 ---
The presence of a chiton that feeds on the alga creates a honeycomb form that rises to 10cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 509 ---
The alga is encrusting and heavily calcified, growing on dead corals and on rocky substrata.

--- SENTENCE 510 ---
The alga is generally smooth to the touch.

--- SENTENCE 511 ---
**Remarks:** Identification of most crustose coralline algae must be made by observing the anatomical features including the reproductive structures.

--- SENTENCE 512 ---
Only a handful of species can be

identified in situ.

--- SENTENCE 513 ---
This is an important alga, providing food for fishes and invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 514 ---
**Distribution:** Tropical to sub-tropical waters.

--- SENTENCE 515 ---
(Weber van Bosse and Foslie) Foslie CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 516 ---
SN: \*Limu ma'a

!

--- SENTENCE 517 ---
This common crustose coralline alga often grows over dead corals, creating a knobby appearance.

--- SENTENCE 518 ---
It is slightly papery in surface texture.

--- SENTENCE 519 ---
It is common in shallow subtidal places or in the inter tidal.

--- SENTENCE 520 ---
The color is purple or pink.

--- SENTENCE 521 ---
This alga often forms rhodoliths, or a small somewhat round rock-like thallus.

--- SENTENCE 522 ---
Tropical to subtropical waters

*Hypnea pannosa* J. Agardh CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 523 ---
SN: \*Limu 'ava

!

--- SENTENCE 524 ---
This common alga is found in shallow places, often near the base of branching corals or in between living corals.

--- SENTENCE 525 ---
The color is yellow in shallow, bright sunny areas and deep red in shaded low light places usually in the subtidal.

--- SENTENCE 526 ---
The alga forms spreading clumps that can float away and establish another community elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 527 ---
It is fairly spongy to the touch, so that when pressed it tends to bounce back.

--- SENTENCE 528 ---
The plant is terete to compressed with many branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 529 ---
They lack spines, which separates it from *Hypnea spinella* (also found

from Samoa).

--- SENTENCE 530 ---
They are anchored by many holdfasts irregularly distributed in places where the plant touches the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 531 ---
Many similar species of *Hypnea* are eaten.

--- SENTENCE 532 ---
The Fijians eat a similar species by boiling it to form a gel and then mixing it with fish (South 1993).

--- SENTENCE 533 ---
Tropical waters.

#### *Hypoglossum caloglossoides* Wynne and Kraft

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 534 ---
This common, epiphytic alga is flat and prostrate.

--- SENTENCE 535 ---
It has a clear node and internode system.

--- SENTENCE 536 ---
The color is usually bright red.

--- SENTENCE 537 ---
It is attached at every node by a complicated rhizoidal mass with secondary attachments coming from marginal cells of the blades.

--- SENTENCE 538 ---
At every node, a pair of blades develops on the surface opposite the rhizoid.

--- SENTENCE 539 ---
The blades are elliptical with a large bell-shaped apical cell.

--- SENTENCE 540 ---
A midrib is another obvious feature.

--- SENTENCE 541 ---
This alga is illustrated

more to show the diversity of marine plants of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 542 ---
It is difficult to see this alga without the aid of a microscope or hand-lens.

--- SENTENCE 543 ---
Tropical waters.

#### *Lithothamnion proliferum* Foslie

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 544 ---
SN: \*Limu patupatu = knobby seaweed.

!

--- SENTENCE 545 ---
This knobby alga is found in deeper waters (5-25m), usually in shaded places, in crevices, and underside of large boulders.

--- SENTENCE 546 ---
It has a glossy pink to red color and a smooth velvet texture.

--- SENTENCE 547 ---
Another distinctive character of this species is the many protuberances arising from the encrusting thallus.

--- SENTENCE 548 ---
The growing margins are sometimes pale red to white color.

--- SENTENCE 549 ---
Sloughing or shedding of the surface layer is observed in this alga.

--- SENTENCE 550 ---
This is one of the few coralline algae that can easily be identified in the field by its knobby or lobed protuberances and texture.

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
Indo-Pacific

#### *Martensia fragilis*

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
Harvey CN: Fragile martensia.

--- SENTENCE 553 ---
This elegant, yet fragile alga is rarely seen.

--- SENTENCE 554 ---
It is common in exposed places along the reef crest, nestled amongst the turf algae.

--- SENTENCE 555 ---
The thallus grows to 5cm tall, but much bigger sizes are reported from other countries.

--- SENTENCE 556 ---
The color varies from an iridescent pink, bluish, orange to magenta.

--- SENTENCE 557 ---
A discoid holdfast attached the plant to the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 558 ---
The terete stipe, about 1mm wide, 3mm long, give rise to a semicircular, fan-shaped delicate blade, about 2cm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 559 ---
The blades are composed of 50-80%

membranous layer, with the other part – a meshwork.

--- SENTENCE 560 ---
The margins are convoluted, rounded with minute teeth.

--- SENTENCE 561 ---
Fertile specimens give a deep red color on the meshwork part (as seen in the photo above - arrows).

--- SENTENCE 562 ---
A delicate plant that is comparable to the petals of a rose.

--- SENTENCE 563 ---
**Distribution:** Tropical to temperate waters.

--- SENTENCE 564 ---
#### *Mastophora pacifica* (Heydrich) Foslie

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 565 ---
This delicate and highly calcified alga is easily crushed when handled.

--- SENTENCE 566 ---
They form overlapping plates on reef walls or in crevices where there is fast water flow.

--- SENTENCE 567 ---
The color varies from light pink to magenta.

--- SENTENCE 568 ---
The blades often aggregate into a round clump.

--- SENTENCE 569 ---
The blades spread to 5cm wide with wavy margins that are slightly raised.

--- SENTENCE 570 ---
The alga is attached by rhizoids

**Remarks:**

--- SENTENCE 571 ---
There are two

species known from the Pacific Islands, with this being the most widespread and common.

--- SENTENCE 572 ---
### *Meristotheca procumbens* Gabrielson & Kraft

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 573 ---
This alga has a rubbery texture and adheres closely to the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 574 ---
The color is brick red color.

--- SENTENCE 575 ---
The expanded flat blades spread to 5cm wide.

--- SENTENCE 576 ---
It is attached by small terete holdfasts.

--- SENTENCE 577 ---
Young algae usually form circular blades becoming irregularly lobed afterwards.

--- SENTENCE 578 ---
Secondary attachments are possible through holdfasts issued from the blade margins.

--- SENTENCE 579 ---
This alga is eaten by the people of Rotuma.

--- SENTENCE 580 ---
It has only been found from Australia, Fiji, Rotuma, Samoa and French Polynesia.

--- SENTENCE 581 ---
This reflects the collecting intensity carried out in these countries.

--- SENTENCE 582 ---
**Distribution:** Pacific Islands.

species belonging to the order Corallinales.

It resembles a fan that is lying on its side.

#### *Portieria hornemannii* (Lyngbye) Silva

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 595 ---
This rarely encountered alga forms bushy clumps on dead corals or other rocky substrata.

--- SENTENCE 596 ---
It is found on the reef flats to shallow subtidal places, usually where there is strong water flow.

--- SENTENCE 597 ---
The color varies from yellow, orange to dark red.

--- SENTENCE 598 ---
It is attached by a discoid holdfast, growing to 15cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 599 ---
The branches are compressed, with tips often curled inwards.

--- SENTENCE 600 ---
This is the first record of this species from the Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 601 ---
Tropical waters

*Prionitis formosana* (Okamura) Kawaguchi & Nguyen CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 602 ---
This stiff and cartilaginous alga grows in places where there is good water flow.

--- SENTENCE 603 ---
It is found in shallow inter tidal places, grooves along the reef crest, and crevices on the fore reef to subtidal places.

--- SENTENCE 604 ---
The color varies from olive green to various shades of red.

--- SENTENCE 605 ---
It is attached by a very tough discoid holdfast, growing to 15cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 606 ---
The thallus is flattened throughout, with the branching immediately arising from near the base.

--- SENTENCE 607 ---
Branching is somewhat dichotomous, although often, adventitious oval branchlets arise irregularly along the margin or from the mid-part of the blade, and

usually more frequent from the mid-to the top part of the plant.

--- SENTENCE 608 ---
Slight constriction is commonly observed at the base of the branches.

--- SENTENCE 609 ---
The thallus is often attached to other parts of the plant giving it strength enabling it to withstand the tidal movement.

--- SENTENCE 610 ---
The blade tips are often rounded or bifid.

--- SENTENCE 611 ---
**Remarks:** Setchell (1924) recorded this alga as edible from Samoa, although to date no record of it being eaten is known.

--- SENTENCE 612 ---
It is rarely collected, probably because of its habitat (exposed places) and cryptic habit.

--- SENTENCE 613 ---
Indo-Pacific

#### *Titanophora weberae*

--- SENTENCE 614 ---
Børgesen

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 615 ---
SN: \*Limu mumu-talatala = roughened red seaweed

!

--- SENTENCE 616 ---
This alga often appears as a ruffled clump that feels slippery yet rough to the touch.

--- SENTENCE 617 ---
It is found in deeper waters at 5-20m depth.

--- SENTENCE 618 ---
The color is white, pink to dark red.

--- SENTENCE 619 ---
Despite its soft thallus, this alga contains calcium carbonate in the interior part.

--- SENTENCE 620 ---
The thallus is complanate to sub-flabellate and is attached by a small disc.

--- SENTENCE 621 ---
A short stipe of about 2mm in diameter bears the expanding blades above.

--- SENTENCE 622 ---
The alga grows to 10cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 623 ---
The blade surface

is endowed with sub-acute excrescences, of which some become lobed branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 624 ---
This alga is best view in its natural habitat where it displays its bright color.

--- SENTENCE 625 ---
It becomes less attractive out of the water and even more so when pressed.

--- SENTENCE 626 ---
Indo-Pacific

### *Tricleocarpa fragilis*

--- SENTENCE 627 ---
(Linnaeus) Huisman & Townsend

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 628 ---
This fragile and beautiful alga is distinctive by its cylindrical thallus that is smooth to the touch.

--- SENTENCE 629 ---
It prefers subtidal places in calm to moderate water flow places.

--- SENTENCE 630 ---
The color is pink, whitish or dark red.

--- SENTENCE 631 ---
It grows to 10cm tall and is attached by a discoid holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 632 ---
The branching is dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 633 ---
The thallus size is uniform throughout.

--- SENTENCE 634 ---
Slight constrictions at dichotomy or at the apices are observed.

--- SENTENCE 635 ---
A depression is obvious at the tip of the thallus and faint striations can be seen on the branches.

--- SENTENCE 636 ---
Two known species have been recorded from the Samoa Archipelago.

#### **PHAEOPHYTA (Brown Algae)**

*Chnoospora minima* (Hering) Papenfuss

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 637 ---
This rarely collected alga grows in the inter tidal zone, usually in exposed places, firmly attached by a discoid holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 638 ---
The thallus is tough, cartilaginous and is light to dark brown, turning black on drying.

--- SENTENCE 639 ---
The erect thallus grows to 8cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 640 ---
The branches are flattened to 1mm wide, irregularly dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 641 ---
The blade tends to broadened at dichotomies and then tapering towards the rounded tips.

--- SENTENCE 642 ---
Small tufts of hairs can be seen on the surface of the blade.

--- SENTENCE 643 ---
**Remarks:** Plants were collected from buoys at the Fagatele Bay and on volcanic rocks, where the wave actions are strong.

--- SENTENCE 644 ---
*Dictyopteris repens* (Okamura) Børgesen CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 645 ---
This small mat-like alga is found mostly as an epiphyte or epilithic.

--- SENTENCE 646 ---
The color is light brown to greenish.

--- SENTENCE 647 ---
It is attached by hair-like filaments emitting from the midrib on the under surface.

--- SENTENCE 648 ---
It creeps on the surface of other large alga, especially near the base.

--- SENTENCE 649 ---
The branching is dichotomous and can be up to 4cm long, with the width up to 3mm wide.

--- SENTENCE 650 ---
This alga is common throughout the year.

--- SENTENCE 651 ---
The larger specimens may be mistaken as a *Dictyota*  species.

--- SENTENCE 652 ---
The presence of the midrib clearly separates this *Dictyopteris* from

#### *Dictyota bartayresiana* Lamoruoux

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 653 ---
SN: \*Limu enaena = brown seaweed

!

--- SENTENCE 654 ---
This bushy alga is abundant during the Southern Hemisphere winter (June-November).

--- SENTENCE 655 ---
It forms compact clumps on dead corals and rubble, usually in the shallow inter tidal places.

--- SENTENCE 656 ---
The color is brown to light green.

--- SENTENCE 657 ---
The branching is dichotomous and it grows to 10cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 658 ---
The width of the branches and thallus is about 3mm.

--- SENTENCE 659 ---
It is attached by hair-like roots issued from the under side of the flat blades.

--- SENTENCE 660 ---
It lacks a midrib.

--- SENTENCE 661 ---
The taxonomy of this alga is confused, and the ultimately placement of Samoan material is tentative until further studies are carried out.

--- SENTENCE 662 ---
Some forms of this alga may resemble *Dictyopteris repens*.

--- SENTENCE 663 ---
But they can be distinguished by the lack of midrib in this species and genus, whereas it is a character of *Dictyopteris*.

--- SENTENCE 664 ---
**Distribution:** Tropical to temperate waters

*Lobophora variegata* (Lamouroux) Womersley CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 665 ---
\*Limu lautaliga-malo = hard earlike seaweed

!

--- SENTENCE 666 ---
This fan-shaped alga is common from the inter tidal zone to deep places.

--- SENTENCE 667 ---
The color is light to dark brown and faint concentric lines can be seen on the surface of the blade.

--- SENTENCE 668 ---
The plant usually lies in the same plane as the substratum, although erect blades are also known.

--- SENTENCE 669 ---
It can reach 10cm tall and up to 8cm wide.

--- SENTENCE 670 ---
The blades often overlap and are attached by fine rhizoids arising from the underside of the blade.

--- SENTENCE 671 ---
This alga grows in many habitats and on all sorts of substrata.

--- SENTENCE 672 ---
Tropical waters

#### *Padina boryana*

--- SENTENCE 674 ---
Thivy

CN: Ear-like seaweed.

--- SENTENCE 675 ---
One of the most distinctive and prominent brown algae found in shallow waters.

--- SENTENCE 676 ---
This usually perennial species appears to be much more in abundance now, possibly due to changes in the marine environment.

--- SENTENCE 677 ---
It forms large beds.

--- SENTENCE 678 ---
Plants are lightly calcified, of large flabellate blades to 10cm high.

--- SENTENCE 679 ---
The blades have the appearance of an ear.

--- SENTENCE 680 ---
Prominent concentric rays are seen on the blade, which is where spores of this alga are found.

--- SENTENCE 681 ---
This alga also has a filamentous form that looks very different from the bladed form.

--- SENTENCE 682 ---
This seaweed is collected by aqua culturists in Samoa to feed trochus.

--- SENTENCE 683 ---
Indo-Pacific

#### *Sargassum anapense* Setchell CN:

--- SENTENCE 684 ---
SN: \*Limu tala

!

--- SENTENCE 685 ---
This alga was named by William Setchell after the village of Agape.

--- SENTENCE 686 ---
It is stiff and adheres strongly to the substratum by a discoid holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 687 ---
It grows in the inter tidal zone and in tide-pools attaining a height of 10cm, whereas to 50cm tall in calm places.

--- SENTENCE 688 ---
The leaves are short and elliptical with short teeth (dentations) along the margin.

--- SENTENCE 689 ---
Small brown spots are scattered along the leaf.

--- SENTENCE 690 ---
A faint midrib can also be seen, usually running to ¾ of the length of the leaf.

--- SENTENCE 691 ---
A tough and short sub-terete stem arises from the disc, bearing up to 5 branches.

--- SENTENCE 692 ---
The leaves arise from the stem in a slight angular disposition.

--- SENTENCE 693 ---
The fruits (reproductive bodies) are found near the top of the branches.

--- SENTENCE 694 ---
They are dark brown and appear like small bunches of bananas, or finger-like projections.

--- SENTENCE 695 ---
**Remarks:** Setchell (1924) noted that this alga is eaten, although this no longer happens.

--- SENTENCE 696 ---
Local fishermen refer to this seaweed as *limu-fa'aleagamea* (destructive weed) as they take over their fishing spots.

--- SENTENCE 697 ---
So far only known from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 698 ---
#### *Turbinaria ornata* Turner

CN: Turban seaweed.

--- SENTENCE 699 ---
SN: \*Limu tala

!

--- SENTENCE 700 ---
This common shallow water alga is very distinct by its turban shaped crown with a double row of teeth.

--- SENTENCE 701 ---
It is very tough and rigid and is firmly attached by a rhizoidal holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 702 ---
The color is olive green, light brown to dark brown.

--- SENTENCE 703 ---
In sheltered places it grows to 15cm, whereas along the reef crest and high impact areas a height of 5cm or less is seen.

--- SENTENCE 704 ---
This alga is closely related to *Sargassum.*

--- SENTENCE 705 ---
It is the only species of this genus reported from the Samoan Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 706 ---
Tropical to subtropical waters.

--- SENTENCE 707 ---

are torn off, it doesn't die.
