### *Boergesenia forbesii* (Harvey) Feldmann

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 727 ---
Limu paluni-meamata = green balloon weed.

!

--- SENTENCE 728 ---
This small but distinctive alga has the appearance of a tiny balloon.

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
It is found often partially covered by sand in shallow waters usually in places where there is moderate water movement.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
It can be found growing in many small communities or rarely as individual.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
The thallus is club-shaped to oval and bright green

color.

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
The vesicle is thin and see-through, and is filled water.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
Plants grow to 5cm tall and are attached by small rhizoids.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
This alga may be confused with *Ventricaria ventricosa* or sailorman's eyeball.

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
The two are different by their thallus size, texture, color and habitat.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
Indo-Pacific

### *Bryopsis pennata* Lamouroux

CN: Bryopsis.

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
This common alga grows in bushy clumps, usually in places where there is moderate to fast water flow.

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
The color is often bright green, or dark green when taken out of the water.

--- SENTENCE 739 ---
It grows to 10cm tall and the thallus resembles a droopy coconut leaf.

--- SENTENCE 740 ---
It is attached by a rhizoidal holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 741 ---
The branches bear smaller branchlets or pinnae that are arranged either secund, whorled or radial, or distichous.

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
This is the only

known species for this genus from the Pacific Islands.

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
*Bryopsis pennata* is a catch all name and that more species are probably lumped under this name.

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
Tropical to subtropical seas.

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
*Caulerpa serrulata* (Forsskål) J. Agardh CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
SN: \*Limu migi = curly weed.

!

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
This common alga is distinguished by its curly or twisted form.

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
The level of curliness is dependant on the habitat, being less curly in sheltered places, and more so in exposed sites.

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
The color is of various shades of green.

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
It grows to more than 10cm tall and has a firm stolon, or prostrate stem.

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
It is attached by very fine but tough roots, which bind strongly to the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
The thallus

has small dentations along the margins.

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
The branching is dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
This alga is found from shallow places to deep waters.

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
It is closely related to the edible sea-grape seaweed (*Caulerpa racemosa*), although this is not eaten.

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
Tropical to subtropical waters.

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
### *Caulerpa peltata* Lamouroux

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
SN: \*Limu pulou = hat weed

!

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
Tropical to subtropical seas.

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
This distinctive alga is found in places where there is fast water flow.

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
It is light to dark green color or slightly iridescent.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
The prostrate stolon bears the erect thallus to 5cm tall, which is swamped with rounded flat discs.

--- SENTENCE 763 ---
The discs are slender and larger in deeper waters, whereas compact and smaller discs are common in exposed shallow places.

--- SENTENCE 764 ---
This alga may be considered a variety of

### *Caulerpa webbiana* Montagne

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 765 ---
This alga is small and can only be appreciated with the aid of a hand-lens or a microscope.

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
It grows to 1cm tall, forming green fuzz along the sandy shores of Olosega and Mafafa villages.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
Larger specimens have been seen from other countries.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
The prostrate stolon bears the erect branches.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
The erect thallus is clothed with branchlets or ramuli that are either arranged in a distichous or whorled manner.

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
It is attached by fine roots issuing from the stolon.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
The ramuli are further forked with two distinctive mucronate tips on the apex.

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
A number of forms are recognized for this alga, of which two are clearly seen in the local materials (form *disticha* as shown in the photo to the left, and *pickeringii*).

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
*Caulerpa cupressoides* (West) C. Agardh

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
[](_page_44_Picture_9.jpeg)

**Distribution:** Tropical to subtropical waters.

--- SENTENCE 775 ---
This alga can be fairly big (to 16cm), although most of the plants collected from American Samoa are small (to 5cm).

--- SENTENCE 776 ---
The prostrate stolon bears the erect branched thallus.

--- SENTENCE 777 ---
The erect thallus lacks branching below but soon forms small busy and sometimes twisted branches.

--- SENTENCE 778 ---
The branches have short teeth-like ramuli that point upwards.

--- SENTENCE 779 ---
The ramuli can be arranged either in a distichous or a 3-dimensional manner.

--- SENTENCE 780 ---
Fine root-like holdfasts peg the alga to the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 781 ---
Three forms of this alga are known from American Samoa.

#### *Caulerpa racemosa* Lamouroux CN: Seagrapes.

--- SENTENCE 782 ---
This common alga is found in various habitats from shallow inter tidal places (shown in the photo above) to subtidal places.

--- SENTENCE 783 ---
It grows on sandy substrata or on hard, carbonate rock.

--- SENTENCE 784 ---
In exposed places it grows close to the substratum, but longer and more elegant in sheltered places attaining a height of 10cm or more.

--- SENTENCE 785 ---
The color is various shades of green, although in some specimens interesting patterns can be seen on individual ramellus.

--- SENTENCE 786 ---
The prostrate stolon is fairly stout bearing filamentous descending roots, whereas erect thalli bear the ramelli.

--- SENTENCE 787 ---
This is one of the best known edible seaweeds in the Pacific and is a delicacy of the Samoans; it is eaten fresh or with coconut meat and green bananas.

--- SENTENCE 788 ---
It is easy to recognize from other *Caulerpa* species by its small rounded or mushroom shaped ramelli.

--- SENTENCE 789 ---
There are over twenty different forms of this species, and some people can easily distinguished the forms by the taste.

--- SENTENCE 790 ---
One of the forms of *C. racemosa* shown above has firm compact ramelli that is very bitter.

--- SENTENCE 791 ---
It leaves a strong bile-like after-taste and is less favored as an edible seaweed.

--- SENTENCE 792 ---
The longer erect thallus bearing softer ramelli is preferred due to its juicy content and slightly sweeter taste that doesn't leave a strong after-taste.

--- SENTENCE 793 ---
Tropical to subtropical places.

--- SENTENCE 794 ---
#### *Caulerpa taxifolia* (Vahl) C. Agardh CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 795 ---
SN: \*Limu launiu = coconut leaf weed

!

--- SENTENCE 796 ---
This elegant alga grows in sandy substratum usually in shallow waters.

--- SENTENCE 797 ---
It is bright green in color.

--- SENTENCE 798 ---
The firm prostrate stolon bears the erect thalli that resemble green coconut leaves.

--- SENTENCE 799 ---
The erect thallus attains a height of 5cm, but 15cm tall specimens are recorded from other countries.

--- SENTENCE 800 ---
The ramelli are arranged in a distichous manner on the erect thallus.

--- SENTENCE 801 ---
The ramelli are cylindrical and elongated tapering to a pointed apex.

--- SENTENCE 802 ---
The length of the ramelli tends to be longer in the middle part of the thallus becoming shorter at the top.

--- SENTENCE 803 ---
This alga has been demonized when it was introduced in places where it normally is not found, proliferating and smothering the natural flora of those places.

--- SENTENCE 804 ---
In tropical waters, this alga is well contained and rarely dominates the flora.

--- SENTENCE 805 ---
**Distribution:** Tropical to temperate waters.

--- SENTENCE 806 ---
*Caulerpa verticillata* J. Agardh CN: Verticillate Caulerpa.

--- SENTENCE 807 ---
A delicate alga that is common but rarely collected.

--- SENTENCE 808 ---
It is found in muddy or soft sandy places, especially near mangroves.

--- SENTENCE 809 ---
It grows in dense communities, which are extremely soft to the touch.

--- SENTENCE 810 ---
The color is light green with dark green tips.

--- SENTENCE 811 ---
Prostrate stolons are small with dense uprights.

--- SENTENCE 812 ---
The uprights bear many fine whorled ramelli in an orderly series, one on top of the other.

--- SENTENCE 813 ---
It grows to 2cm tall in materials collected so far from American Samoa,

whereas to 7cm in other places.

--- SENTENCE 814 ---
This alga is yet to be found in the western parts of the Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 815 ---
It was found abundant in the shallow muddy substratum of Pala Lagoon, near the Nu'uuli Airport.

--- SENTENCE 816 ---
*Caulerpella ambigua* (Okamura) Prud'homme van Reine & Lokhorst

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 817 ---
This small alga has a weakly developed stolon.

--- SENTENCE 818 ---
It grows to 7mm tall.

--- SENTENCE 819 ---
It is attached to the substratum by short branched rhizoids.

--- SENTENCE 820 ---
The branching is somewhat dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 821 ---
The ramelli are arranged either distichous or whorled, and are rounded at the apices.

--- SENTENCE 822 ---
The ramelli size is variable but frequently having a series of longest pairs at bottom then successively shorter ones, then reverting back to longer series.

--- SENTENCE 823 ---
Some parts of the

uprights are devoid of ramelli.

--- SENTENCE 824 ---
A rarely encountered alga that is probably common but very difficult to see due to its small size.

--- SENTENCE 825 ---
Tropical waters.

### *Chlorodesmis fastigiata* (C. Agardh)

--- SENTENCE 826 ---
Ducker

CN: Turtle weed.

--- SENTENCE 827 ---
SN: Limu laumei

!

--- SENTENCE 828 ---
A common alga that is also very conspicuous due to its fine form and bright green color.

--- SENTENCE 829 ---
It is found in places with strong currents especially along the reef crest and algal ridge zone.

--- SENTENCE 830 ---
It is attached by a compact rhizoidal holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 831 ---
It grows to 10cm tall, forming small bushes.

--- SENTENCE 832 ---
The fine filaments are loose and divide dichotomously.

--- SENTENCE 833 ---
A slight constriction is always found on the base of each dichotomy.

--- SENTENCE 834 ---
Some filaments are moniliform.

--- SENTENCE 835 ---
**Remarks:** known as turtle weed, although turtles are not known to feed on this unpalatable alga.

--- SENTENCE 836 ---
Tropical waters.

#### *Codium bulbopilum* Setchell

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 837 ---
SN: \*Limu meamata = green weed.

!

--- SENTENCE 838 ---
This cryptic alga grows in shallow places where the water flow is moderate to fast.

--- SENTENCE 839 ---
It forms clumps and has a very prostrate mode of growth.

--- SENTENCE 840 ---
It is often covered with sand or silt.

--- SENTENCE 841 ---
The branching is irregular or dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 842 ---
It has a spongy texture and is dark green color, occasionally with light green tips.

--- SENTENCE 843 ---
The alga forms large meadows, spreading over a 10m sandy field.

--- SENTENCE 844 ---
The individual thallus is about 3-5mm wide.

--- SENTENCE 845 ---
This alga or a similar

species is eaten by Fijians.

--- SENTENCE 846 ---
It makes a great addition to a stir-fry vegetable dish, having a crunchy, nutty flavor.

--- SENTENCE 847 ---
The taxonomy of this alga, which Setchell described from Lauli'i, Tutuila Island, is still unresolved, being closely similar to *Codium geppiorum*.

--- SENTENCE 848 ---
#### *Dictyosphaeria versluysii* Weber-van Bosse

CN: Button weed.

--- SENTENCE 849 ---
SN: \*Limu fa'amau

!

--- SENTENCE 850 ---
This is a common alga in Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 851 ---
It tightly adheres to where it grows, usually on rock in strong current places.

--- SENTENCE 852 ---
The color is pale green, almost opaque in some plants.

--- SENTENCE 853 ---
It is tough, globular to compressed, fairly solid cushions, to 5cm wide.

--- SENTENCE 854 ---
The surface of the cells is smooth and polygonal.

--- SENTENCE 855 ---
It is attached by small rhizoids on the bottom side of the alga.

--- SENTENCE 856 ---
It grows either solitary or often in groups, overlapping each

other.

--- SENTENCE 857 ---
The young thallus resembles a small green button, often rounded in

shape.

--- SENTENCE 858 ---
Tropical waters.

#### *Dictyosphaeria cavernosa* (Forsskål) Børgesen

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 859 ---
This uncommon alga is found in shallow inter tidal places.

--- SENTENCE 860 ---
Its size is 5cm, although much bigger plants to 20cm or more have been recorded from other countries.

--- SENTENCE 861 ---
It is closely related to *D. versluysii*

--- SENTENCE 862 ---
but they can be separated by the hollow thallus in *D. cavernosa*.

--- SENTENCE 863 ---
This is quite different from the firm *D. versluysii*.

--- SENTENCE 864 ---
Another possible distinguishing character is the bubble like appearance on the surface of the alga.

--- SENTENCE 865 ---
**Remarks:** Considered a

nuisance to some parts of Hawaii, aggressively competing with corals.

--- SENTENCE 866 ---
This is one of the few examples of local flora causing negative ecological impacts to the environment; an issue that is often attributed to introduced species.

--- SENTENCE 867 ---
Tropical to subtropical waters.

--- SENTENCE 868 ---
*Enteromorpha intestinalis* (Linnaeus) Nees] CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 869 ---
This alga is light green to yellow color.

--- SENTENCE 870 ---
It is common in the high inter tidal zone, along pillars of bridges, or in mangrove environments.

--- SENTENCE 871 ---
Initially it forms little clumps that attach firmly to the substratum, later becoming freefloating covering a big area, usually in sheltered places where the environment is not pristine.

--- SENTENCE 872 ---
This alga provides a good indicator on the status of the environment.

--- SENTENCE 873 ---
It absorbs heavy metals and other pollutants keeping the marine environment relatively clean.

--- SENTENCE 874 ---
This alga is eaten is some places, including Fiji.

--- SENTENCE 875 ---
The taxonomy of *Enteromorpha* has recently been updated by Hayden *et al.* (2003).

--- SENTENCE 876 ---
The genus is now been relegated to the older name *Ulva*.

--- SENTENCE 877 ---
**Distribution:** Tropical to temperate places.

--- SENTENCE 878 ---
Top –growing on a rock near a river.

--- SENTENCE 879 ---
Middle –growing on the side of a fishing vessel.

--- SENTENCE 880 ---
Bottom – being offered for sale at Suva market, Fiji.*

It is found in deeper waters and has larger segments that are lax.

#### *Halimeda opuntia* **(Linnaeus) Lamoroux**

CN: Halimeda. SN: Limu

!

--- SENTENCE 902 ---
A common alga that forms large beds in shallow waters (as seen in above photo).

--- SENTENCE 903 ---
The color is green-white.

--- SENTENCE 904 ---
This alga very straggly in appearance and is attached to the substratum by many holdfasts.

--- SENTENCE 905 ---
It can grow up to 10cm tall, and form communities as wide as 100m. Individual plants resemble flexible flattened string of beads.

--- SENTENCE 906 ---
It is lightly calcified, with colorless siphons or filaments throughout the segments.

--- SENTENCE 907 ---
Each segment is tri-lobed to many lobes, but often resembling a duck's foot.

--- SENTENCE 908 ---
The surface of the segments is very important in

identifying the different species.

--- SENTENCE 909 ---
The arrangement of outermost siphons (or utricles) gives characters to the surface of the segments.

--- SENTENCE 910 ---
Some segments have hexagonal shaped surfaces, whereas others are circular.

--- SENTENCE 911 ---
Some surface utricles are closely packed whereas others are loosely arranged.

--- SENTENCE 912 ---
The utricles are another important feature in identifying the species.

--- SENTENCE 913 ---
Some utricles divide in two, whereas others remain entire throughout.

--- SENTENCE 914 ---
The skeleton of dead *Halimeda opuntia* contributes significantly to the substratum in many tropical reefs (especially in atoll countries).

--- SENTENCE 915 ---
Furthermore, it is eaten by some animals (e.g. parrotfish, triggerfish) and invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 916 ---
Howe

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 917 ---
This beautiful alga is often found in small clusters on the sheltered side of rock, rubble and other large structures in shallow waters.

--- SENTENCE 918 ---
The color is light green to whitish on the lower side becoming darker green near the top.

--- SENTENCE 919 ---
The cylindrical oftenarching body is fairly delicate and grows to 2-5cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 920 ---
The tissue is impregnated with calcium carbonate, especially near the base.

--- SENTENCE 921 ---
A honeycomb like pattern is a characteristic of this alga.

--- SENTENCE 922 ---
Fine filaments are found near the top.

--- SENTENCE 923 ---
It is distinguished from *Neomeris annulata* by the lack of annular rings, which are more pronounced near the base of the latter.

--- SENTENCE 924 ---
The reproductive structures are in compartments that are protected by calcium carbonate.

--- SENTENCE 925 ---
This alga contributes to the sediment.

--- SENTENCE 926 ---
It is also consumed by invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 927 ---
Littler and Littler (2003) reported *Neomeris dumetosa*

--- SENTENCE 928 ---
Lamouroux from American Samoa (a Caribbean species).

--- SENTENCE 929 ---
Examination of their material is needed to assess the presence of this Caribbean alga in the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 930 ---
#### *Rhipidosiphon javensis* Montagne

CN: Fan weed.

--- SENTENCE 931 ---
SN: Limu ili-meamata = Green fan weed.

!

--- SENTENCE 932 ---
This irregularly fan-shaped alga is found on the sheltered side of large boulders, from subtidal to lower inter tidal places.

--- SENTENCE 933 ---
The siphonous filaments are joined by calcium carbonate to form the fan shaped blade that is one cell thick.

--- SENTENCE 934 ---
Each filament is dichotomously divided with a slight pinch at the base of the filament.

--- SENTENCE 935 ---
It is attached by a rhizoidal holdfast that penetrates the substratum; the rest of the thallus generally hangs downward, although erect plants are common in smaller rubble and rock.

--- SENTENCE 936 ---
Often missed during ecological surveys because of its small size and cryptic nature, this alga is one of the many calcified green algae.

--- SENTENCE 937 ---
Found in tropical waters.

#### *Tydemania expeditionis* Weber-van Bosse

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 938 ---
This alga has two distinct forms.

--- SENTENCE 939 ---
The flabelloid form (fan-shape) is the only known morph from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 940 ---
The verticillate form (whorls) is spongy and resembles beads; is found throughout the western Pacific to Fiji.

--- SENTENCE 941 ---
This alga is grayish green and grows to 20cm long.

--- SENTENCE 942 ---
Branching is somewhat dichotomous.

--- SENTENCE 943 ---
The flabelloid form is built like a series of fans, with each successive series on top of older ones.

--- SENTENCE 944 ---
Tough siphons run throughout each of the series.

--- SENTENCE 945 ---
Calcium carbonate is deposited between the siphons.

--- SENTENCE 946 ---
It is found hanging from walls or on the side of large boulders, at depths of 2-20m.

**Remarks:**

--- SENTENCE 947 ---
According to past studies (Coppejans and Prud'homme van Reine 1989), the flabellate form is found in shaded places as seen from Samoan plants, whereas the verticillate forms are found in high light places.

--- SENTENCE 948 ---
The typical form (flabelloid) of this alga collected from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 949 ---
The verticillate form, which is yet to be found in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 950 ---
This form has been collected throughout Southeast Asia and to Fiji.

#### *Valonia fastigiata* Harvey ex J. Agardh

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 951 ---
SN: \*Limu patupatu meamata = green bubble seaweed.

!

--- SENTENCE 952 ---
This beautiful alga forms clumps in sandy places.

--- SENTENCE 953 ---
It is often covered with sand.

--- SENTENCE 954 ---
The surface appears to be made of bubbles tightly packed together.

--- SENTENCE 955 ---
When teased, a distinctive branching arrangement can be observed, of elongated round utricles cutting off more utricles on the top.

--- SENTENCE 956 ---
This character separates it with *Valonia aegagropila* whose branching is disorderly.

--- SENTENCE 957 ---
The color is light to dark green.

--- SENTENCE 958 ---
It forms clumps from 10cm to 1m wide.

--- SENTENCE 959 ---
The erect thallus to 5- 10cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 960 ---
The presence of calcium carbonate on the

surface of this alga makes it suitable for coralline and turf algae to settle and grow on it.

--- SENTENCE 961 ---
Tropical waters.

This interesting alga is rarely collected due to its cryptic habitat.

--- SENTENCE 965 ---

#### *Ventricaria ventricosa*

CN: Sailorman's eyeball.

--- SENTENCE 967 ---
SN: \*Limu paluni

!

--- SENTENCE 968 ---
This alga is easily identified by its balloon like structure.

--- SENTENCE 969 ---
It is spherical to sub-spherical and filled with water, growing either solitary or in small clusters.

--- SENTENCE 970 ---
The alga is attached by minute rhizoids at the base.

--- SENTENCE 971 ---
It is found from the inter tidal to subtidal, usually wedged between crevices or on rocky substrata.

--- SENTENCE 972 ---
The thallus size is variable and can attain over 10cm tall and 10cm wide.

--- SENTENCE 973 ---
When sailors gazed into the clear tropical waters they were shocked to see what appeared to be an eyeball staring back at them; hence the name Sailorman's eyeball.

--- SENTENCE 974 ---
This alga provides an ideal substratum for the red crustose coralline alga (*Hydrolithon farinosum*).

--- SENTENCE 975 ---
In tropical waters.

### **CYANOPHYTA (blue-green algae)**

*Brachytrichia quoyi* (C. Agardh) Bornet & Flahault CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 976 ---
Forming spongy cushions on volcanic rocks in wave splashed areas.

--- SENTENCE 977 ---
The color is dark green when wet to black when dry.

--- SENTENCE 978 ---
It often is hollow and can easily be plied off the rock.

--- SENTENCE 979 ---
The rounded cells appear like a chain of beads with an elongated tail-like end.

--- SENTENCE 980 ---

--- SENTENCE 981 ---
This unassuming alga is similar to the red

algal groups, having branched filaments.

--- SENTENCE 982 ---
It is fairly common from the high inter tidal places.

--- SENTENCE 983 ---
The given illustration is of a section made from an algal specimen and stained with aniline blue.

Tropical to subtropical waters.

### *Symplocca hydnoides* (Harvey)

--- SENTENCE 986 ---
Kützing

CN: Not known.

--- SENTENCE 987 ---
This distinctive alga is stiff and bushy in appearance.

--- SENTENCE 988 ---
It is brown to maroon color and usually with a wider mid-portion tapering towards the top.

--- SENTENCE 989 ---

#### **MAGNOLIOPHYTA (seagrasses)** *Halophila ovalis* (Brown) Hooker

CN: Paddle weed.

--- SENTENCE 993 ---
SN: Limu foe

!

--- SENTENCE 994 ---
This common seagrass is found in shallow waters, often exposed during low tides.

--- SENTENCE 995 ---
It is very distinctive by its oval or paddle shape leaf.

--- SENTENCE 996 ---
It has a distinctive rhizome (prostrate running stem) that is vital for its growth.

--- SENTENCE 997 ---
It is attached by filamentous roots that bind loose sand and debris together.

--- SENTENCE 998 ---
The erect portion is 5cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 999 ---
This seagrass is similar to *Halophila minor*

--- SENTENCE 1000 ---
and they may have been incorrectly identified as *H. ovalis* in

the past.

--- SENTENCE 1001 ---
The number of veins (10-25) remains the only morphological character separating the two species.

--- SENTENCE 1002 ---
Whether this is a tenable character remains to be seen.

--- SENTENCE 1003 ---
One of the three species found in Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1004 ---
Tropical to subtropical Indo-Pacific waters.

--- SENTENCE 1005 ---
*Halophila minor* (Zollinger) Hartog CN: Paddle weed.

--- SENTENCE 1006 ---
SN: Limu foe.

!

--- SENTENCE 1007 ---
Very similar to *Halophila ovalis*.

--- SENTENCE 1008 ---
The leaves are generally smaller, although it often overlaps with the leaf size of *H. ovalis.*

--- SENTENCE 1009 ---
As mentioned earlier, the two are currently separated by the number of cross veins with 8 or less in this species, compared to 10- 25 in *H. ovalis*.

--- SENTENCE 1010 ---
The erect part is 3cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 1011 ---
This species is less common.

--- SENTENCE 1012 ---
Indo-Pacific

waters.

### *3.2 Algae per Site*

Six-hundred and thirty-three specimens were analyzed comprising 239 species (Appendix 1).

--- SENTENCE 1013 ---
The majority of the flora belonged to the Rhodophyta with 133 taxa.

--- SENTENCE 1014 ---
Sixty Chlorophyta taxa were enumerated.

--- SENTENCE 1015 ---
The Phaeophyta comprised 29 taxa, and 27 Cyanophyta and only two species of seagrasses (Magnoliophyta) were found.

--- SENTENCE 1016 ---
Twenty-six sites were surveyed (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 1017 ---
Utulei Point had the highest number of species with 56 recorded (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 1018 ---
The Fagatele Bay had the second highest with 50 species and Fagasa, Onososopo, Mafafa, Aua, Fagaalu and Nu'utele village were other sites with high diversity.

--- SENTENCE 1019 ---
The least diverse sites were the Dock 1 and Nu'usilaelae Island both with 8 species.

## **4.0 DISCUSSION**

### *4.1 Floral diversity of American Samoa*

The term marine floral diversity encompasses the many plants that are found in the marine environment.

--- SENTENCE 1038 ---
Macro-floral diversity specifically targets species that can be seen without the use of lenses, microscopes or other devices.

--- SENTENCE 1039 ---
The marine flora includes algae, cyanophytes or cyanobacteria, diatoms and higher plants (e.g. mangroves and seagrasses).

--- SENTENCE 1040 ---
The surveys undertaken in American Samoa focused on five phyla, which are the often-encountered groups when marine ecological surveys are undertaken.

--- SENTENCE 1041 ---
It is estimated that close to a third of the taxa collected falls within the macro-flora criteria; although, this estimation may be conservative if one considers obvious algal communities that are composed of many small species.

--- SENTENCE 1042 ---
The 239 species recorded from these surveys showed a fairly diverse flora for American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1043 ---
This so far probably comprised a 60% of the total flora, estimated at 400 species.

--- SENTENCE 1044 ---
An increase of more than 200% is seen from the surveys compared to that undertaken in the early 1920s (Setchell 1924), and the more recent surveys by Birkeland et al. (1987; 1995) – refer to Figure 1 and 2.

--- SENTENCE 1045 ---
This increase in the number of species is attributed to extensive sites surveyed, improved collecting techniques (e.g. the use of SCUBA) and improved taxonomy.

--- SENTENCE 1046 ---
Surveys of varying habitats from subtidal, inter tidal to salt spray have also yielded species not previously collected.

--- SENTENCE 1047 ---
Graph showing the number of species per the four major algal groups during the surveys of William Setchell, Birkeland and others, and this study.

!

--- SENTENCE 1048 ---
Graph showing the total number of taxa found in the past three studies (Setchell; Birkeland *et al.*, and this study).

--- SENTENCE 1049 ---
## *4.2 Site and species diversity*

Utulei Point had the highest number of species (56) of the 26 sites surveyed (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 1050 ---
The site is composed of an undulating reef flat, extending *ca.*

--- SENTENCE 1051 ---
50m from the shoreline to the edge of the fringing reef.

--- SENTENCE 1052 ---
The substratum is of coral rubble, coarse sand and consolidated carbonate structure.

--- SENTENCE 1053 ---
Some volcanic rocks are also found.

--- SENTENCE 1054 ---
Some deeper pits (3-5m depth) with a fine sandy substratum are also found.

--- SENTENCE 1055 ---
Moreover, rubble banks, remnants of past cyclones occur.

--- SENTENCE 1056 ---
The fine sandy pits were dominated by soft-corals and *Halophila ovalis*.

--- SENTENCE 1057 ---
Utulei is located closer to the harbor entrance and with a moderate current flow.

--- SENTENCE 1058 ---
The current flow and the varied habitats, such as the vertical and walls with some slopes, reef flats and sandy pits may contribute to the high diversity.

--- SENTENCE 1059 ---
Industrial developments along the foreshore include the sewerage plant and fuel storage depot and these may contribute nutrients to the environment enhancing algal growth.

--- SENTENCE 1060 ---
Surface water run-off from the steep mountain slopes is another potential nutrient enhancement source.

--- SENTENCE 1061 ---
The Fagatele National Marine Sanctuary bay provided the next highest diversity of algae, with 50 species recorded.

--- SENTENCE 1062 ---
This site is fairly pristine with very low human impact.

--- SENTENCE 1063 ---
The current flow is strong and the water is flushed from the bay daily.

--- SENTENCE 1064 ---
There are large scattered boulders and bommies throughout the site and a relatively short reef-flat.

--- SENTENCE 1065 ---
Waves pound on the cliffs along the edge of the bay, creating a unique algal community along the spray zone.

--- SENTENCE 1066 ---
With large volcanic rocks along the shoreline, small algal communities thrive on the leeward side.

--- SENTENCE 1067 ---
These varied conditions and microhabitats are ideal, supporting diverse algal communities.

--- SENTENCE 1068 ---
The least diverse sites were Nu'usilaelae Island and the main Dock in the Pago Pago Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 1069 ---
Nu'usilaelae Island is located on the seaward side of the Ofu village reef-flat.

--- SENTENCE 1070 ---
It is an inhospitable place, where the waves pound higher than the volcanic mass and the water flow around it is treacherous.

--- SENTENCE 1071 ---
The low diversity of Nu'usilaelae is attributed to the limited and inadequate collection.

--- SENTENCE 1072 ---
The Dock site is fairly murky with green water.

--- SENTENCE 1073 ---
Visibility was about 2m. Collecting was confined to the pilings and rocks along the wharf.

--- SENTENCE 1074 ---
The low diversity of this site is most probably due to the poor condition of the waters.

--- SENTENCE 1075 ---
The most widely abundant alga was *Dictyosphaeria versluysii* recorded from 70% of the sites.

--- SENTENCE 1076 ---
This alga is found in most habitats and is hardy; able to withstand being exposed during low tides as well as in extremely exposed places.

--- SENTENCE 1077 ---
Although it was recorded from 18 of the 26 sites, in reality this alga is probably found in all of the sites, except the Dock (site 4).

--- SENTENCE 1078 ---
*Gelidiopsis repens* was the next widely abundant alga recorded from 58% of the sites.

--- SENTENCE 1079 ---
This alga forms turf communities in subtidal places, although it is also abundant in the inter tidal zone.

--- SENTENCE 1080 ---
It can withstand strong water flow, as well as calm waters.

--- SENTENCE 1081 ---
Other commonly encountered algae include *Amphiroa foliacea, Bryopsis pennata, Cheilosporum spectabile* and *Chlorodesmis fastigiata*.

--- SENTENCE 1082 ---
One of the rarely encountered macroalgae is *Halymenia durvillei*, where it so far has only been recorded from sites within Pago Pago Harbor (Utulei and Fagatogo).

--- SENTENCE 1083 ---
*Acanthophora spicifera* is common throughout the Pacific Islands, but was only collected from one site (Nu'utele Island).

--- SENTENCE 1084 ---
This alga is regarded as a recent introduction to Hawaii, and has caused negative impacts to the native flora.

--- SENTENCE 1085 ---
In Fiji, it is known as *lumi wawa* and is one of about 10 seaweed species eaten there.

--- SENTENCE 1086 ---
Utulei had a very high diversity of green, brown and red algae.

--- SENTENCE 1087 ---
However, Mafafa on Ofu Island had the greatest number of green algal species (22 spp).

--- SENTENCE 1088 ---
Fagatele Bay recorded the highest number of red algal species (31 spp), followed closely by Fagasa with 29 species and Utulei (27 spp).

--- SENTENCE 1089 ---
Two sites shared the highest diversity of brown algae both with six species each (Fagatele Bay and Ofu village).

--- SENTENCE 1090 ---
Utulei had the next highest diversity of brown algae with five species.

--- SENTENCE 1091 ---
Mafafa has a fairly short reef flat and a narrow treacherous channel.

--- SENTENCE 1092 ---
Despite this high floral diversity, there remains a big need for further taxonomic (including monographic treatment) and ecological studies on particular groups such as the Cyanophyta and the Corallinales of the Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 1093 ---
The taxonomy of Cyanophyta remains contentious and species from the tropical oceans are poorly studied.

--- SENTENCE 1094 ---
Researchers in tropical places continue to rely on results of studies undertaken in cold-water places.

--- SENTENCE 1095 ---
Although cyanophytes are known to be widespread or cosmopolitan in distribution, one is always dubious when stating that a particular species found in a 'hot' tide-pool is the same as that found in freezing cold waters.

--- SENTENCE 1096 ---
The treatment here is tentative and follows the latest work or compilation of works from around the Pacific and in other tropical places (Littler and Littler 2000, 2003; Tseng 1984).

--- SENTENCE 1097 ---
The Corallinales are an important group in coral reefs because of their varied roles.

--- SENTENCE 1098 ---
They provide food for many invertebrates and fishes; they consolidate the loose substratum such as rubble; they are good indicators of a healthy ecosystem; they complement the varied colors that are the hallmark of tropical coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1099 ---
The taxonomy of Corallinales is less contentious than that of Cyanophyta.

--- SENTENCE 1100 ---
However, special attention must be accorded when dealing with corallines, and one cannot simply hack them off the reef and put them in the collecting bag along with other fleshy algae.

--- SENTENCE 1101 ---
The colors are useful, but not accurate in their identification.

--- SENTENCE 1102 ---
The growing tips (along the margins) are important in identification and care must be taken that the growing margins are not damaged in their removal and transportation.

--- SENTENCE 1103 ---
Moreover, reproductive structures must be collected.

--- SENTENCE 1104 ---
This can be a challenge as the reproductive structures are usually small and difficult to see, except in a few species (eg. *Mastophora*), and corallines tend to prefer exposed habitats or places where the water movement is fast.

--- SENTENCE 1105 ---
In many of the sites surveyed, coralline algae were the most dominant algal group often the most abundant life-form along the substratum.

--- SENTENCE 1106 ---
It is highly recommended that a more concerted effort to fully identify and provide a good guide of the corallines of American Samoa be pursued.

--- SENTENCE 1107 ---
This should prove valuable to coral reef researchers undertaking monitoring in the islands.

### *4.3 Invasive species*

One of the purposes of these surveys was also to assess the flora for potential invasive or introduced species.

--- SENTENCE 1108 ---
American Samoa has been fortunate that algal studies had been undertaken in the Archipelago in the late 1800s and specifically on the island in 1920s.

--- SENTENCE 1109 ---
Hence, some baseline data are available for comparative purposes.

--- SENTENCE 1110 ---
The continuous marine plant surveys being undertaken by the author in neighboring Samoa islands further provide data useful in understanding the floral composition of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1111 ---
The majority of the new additions consist of minute epiphytic algae such as *Herposiphonia secunda, Griffithsia subcylindrica, Dictyopteris repens* and *Hypoglossum anomalum*.

--- SENTENCE 1112 ---
Some larger seaweeds are also found from these surveys including *Halymenia durvillei* (20 cm tall), *Caulerpa serrulata* (to 10 cm tall) and *Galaxaura filamentosa* (8 cm tall).

--- SENTENCE 1113 ---
There has been an increased abundance of *Halymenia durvillei* and *Caulerpa serrulata* at the Apia Harbor, Western Samoa (pers.

--- SENTENCE 1114 ---
obs.) and both algae were collected from Utulei site near the Harbor entrance.

--- SENTENCE 1115 ---
It is reasonable to assume that the two algae may have been introduced into Pago Pago Harbor from neighboring Apia Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 1116 ---
The frequent commuting between the harbors by fishing vessels, commercial ships and other boats may have led to this scenario.

--- SENTENCE 1117 ---
In saying this, however, it is paramount that more studies will need to be undertaken, including molecular work, to confirm this.

--- SENTENCE 1118 ---
*Galaxaura filamentosa* is a red alga that is often covered in fine silt, thus could have been easily overlooked.

--- SENTENCE 1119 ---
It was collected from all of the sites except the Docks; thus it is considered part of the native flora.

--- SENTENCE 1120 ---
The green algae, *Tydemania expeditionis, Valonia macrophysa, Parvocaulis parvula, P. exigua, Sporocladopsis erythraea, Dictyosphaeria cavernosa, Codium mamillosum*  and *Boergesenia forbesii* are new records for American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1121 ---
With the exception of *Sporocladopsis erythraea* and *Codium mamillosum* all of the other Chlorophyta have been recorded from neighboring Western Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1122 ---
The minute epiphytic alga, *Sporocladpsis erythraea* is a new record for the Archipelago and it certainly has a disjunct distribution.

--- SENTENCE 1123 ---
The alga is one of two known species of the genus that was described from the Red Sea by Nasr (1944).

--- SENTENCE 1124 ---
The other species *Sporocaldopsis novae-zealandiae* is found in New Zealand and Australia (Millar & Kraft 1994).

--- SENTENCE 1125 ---
The occurrence of this alga in the American Samoan flora is interesting, especially as it was epiphytic on the base of a *Sargassum anapense* an endemic brown alga to Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1126 ---
Furthermore, the *Sargassum* and its host were collected from a site (Amalau) that is considered of low impact from potential invasives.

--- SENTENCE 1127 ---
This alga is probably not an introduced species, but may have been overlooked due to its small and inconspicuous status.

--- SENTENCE 1128 ---
Of the six sites that are considered vulnerable to introduced species (Aua, Docks 1 & 2, Leloaloa, Onososopo and Utulei), the algal flora was found to be very similar to sites from less vulnerable places.

--- SENTENCE 1129 ---
Only one alga *Grateloupia filicina* was found to be an anomaly in the flora, although it has been reported from Fiji (South & Skelton 2004), Hawaii (Abbott 1999) and from French Polynesia (Payri & N'Yeurt 1997).

--- SENTENCE 1130 ---
This alga could be considered a recent introduction as it was only found at Dock 1 attached to a rope that was anchoring a landing craft.

--- SENTENCE 1131 ---
This is the first record of this species from the Archipelago.

### *4.4 Endemism*

There are only a few cases where endemic marine plants are known, and one such case is a green alga, *Avrainvillea rotumensis* recorded only from Rotuma Island, north of Fiji (N'Yeurt 1996).

--- SENTENCE 1132 ---
The isolation of Rotuma Island may have led to the endemic nature of this alga.

--- SENTENCE 1133 ---
Two American Samoan brown algae were described by Setchell in 1924, *Sargassum anapense* and *Sargassum fonanonensis* (named after the villages of Agape and Fogagogo, respectively), and have never been described elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 1134 ---
Very similar plants were collected by the author in neighboring Samoa, and were found to conform to Setchell's plants.

--- SENTENCE 1135 ---
But these were also found to match two other species, *Sargassum crassifolium* and *Sargassum cristaefolium*, that are found throughout the Indo-Pacific waters.

--- SENTENCE 1136 ---
Whether the American Samoan plants are endemic remains to be seen, and can only be resolved with further studies and comparisons with materials from neighboring islands.

### *4.5 American Samoan flora versus other Pacific Islands*

The American Samoa flora conforms to the biogeography theory of decreasing diversity from the Indo-Pacific centre of biodiversity, as shown in Table 3.

--- SENTENCE 1137 ---
The high algal diversity for tropical Pacific regions can be found in northern part of Australia, Micronesia and Melanesia.

--- SENTENCE 1138 ---
The diversity gradually reduces going eastward to French Polynesia.

--- SENTENCE 1139 ---
The American Samoa flora (222 species from the three phyla) is richer compared to Cook Islands and Easter Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1140 ---
The French Polynesian flora shows a high diversity and this is attributed to a higher collecting effort by a resident phycologist.

--- SENTENCE 1141 ---
Seasonal variation of the French Polynesian flora as well as access to habitats that are often inhospitable may yield species that can be missed or overlooked during short surveys, such as this.

--- SENTENCE 1142 ---
The Palolo Deep Marine Reserve flora contains about 80% of the total number of species recorded from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1143 ---
The Suva Barrier Reef, Fiji contains over 92% of the species number to that of the current flora.

--- SENTENCE 1144 ---
This indicates that there still is considerable scope for the American Samoa flora to increase the species diversity to a similar number to that of the French Polynesian flora.

--- SENTENCE 1145 ---
Personal communications with recent algal collectors from American Samoa, have indicated new additions to the flora including a possibly new species of *Ceramium* and a *Wrangelia anastomosans,* an alga so far only recorded from Micronesia (Peter Vroom pers.

--- SENTENCE 1146 ---
The epiphytes and other non-macro algal flora are inadequately treated and as much as 100 species are omitted.

--- SENTENCE 1147 ---
The flora of selected Pacific Island countries, based on the three algal phyla (Chlorophyta, Phaeophyta and Rhodophyta).

### *4.6 Recommendations*

The diversity of the marine plants of American Samoa is better known through these biological surveys, and those made by previous workers.

--- SENTENCE 1158 ---
The following recommendations will help continue this excellent track of coral reef biodiversity surveys of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1159 ---
#### *Compilation of algal surveys*

A comprehensive compilation of all algal collections, literature and data specific to American Samoa needs to be undertaken.

--- SENTENCE 1160 ---
There have been many itinerant algal collectors and expeditions passing through the islands, who have taken specimens to their institutions or their personal herbaria.

--- SENTENCE 1161 ---
These collectors and expeditions need to be identified and a database designed to store them, so that future researchers are aware of previously undertaken work.

--- SENTENCE 1162 ---
There are records of marine plants from the Archipelago being reported in the literature.

--- SENTENCE 1163 ---
Some of the literature is obscure and difficult to obtain, or may be found in personal libraries.

--- SENTENCE 1164 ---
A concerted effort to identify, copy and obtain these references is recommended, and that they be kept in places where local researchers can access them.

--- SENTENCE 1165 ---
The need to have a dedicated database where the marine biodiversity is stored should be considered paramount.

--- SENTENCE 1166 ---
It is strongly urged that authorities consider building such a database using information that has been obtained in this and previous studies.

#### *Field guide to American Samoan marine plants*

This report illustrates 67 species of marine plants found during the surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1167 ---
This represents a small part of the overall flora of the islands.

--- SENTENCE 1168 ---
It is therefore recommended that consideration be given in developing a comprehensive field guide.

--- SENTENCE 1169 ---
The field guide should be in simple language, both in English and Samoan and it should have useful and interesting information capable of raising awareness of the importance of marine plants on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1170 ---
Such a field guide would be useful in the tourism industry as well as an important reference for schools.

--- SENTENCE 1171 ---
#### *Ecological monitoring*

American Samoa has been at the forefront of coral reef monitoring.

--- SENTENCE 1172 ---
The well-known transect undertaken by Alfred Major in the 1920s provides vital baseline information on the reef structure.

--- SENTENCE 1173 ---
Subsequent coral reef workers have used Major's transect to compare the health of the reef in the 1920s with that of today.

--- SENTENCE 1174 ---
Marine plants are an important part of the coral reef ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 1175 ---
They are often the dominant organisms as seen from Fagatele National Marine Sanctuary.

--- SENTENCE 1176 ---
Through this survey, the data on marine plants should be useful when ecological surveys are undertaken.

--- SENTENCE 1177 ---
It is important for ecologists and coral reef monitors to start monitoring the dominant species, rather than life-form categories.

#### *Expert studies of problematic groups*

Coralline algae and cyanophytes are important marine plants in American Samoa, with the former contributing to reef structure and stability, and the latter important indicators of eutropihication, and also functioning as nitrogen-fixers.

--- SENTENCE 1178 ---
Because of limited time, these two groups have not been thoroughly treated here.

--- SENTENCE 1179 ---
Their taxonomy, in particular the latter group, remains controversial.

--- SENTENCE 1180 ---
A detailed treatment of these two groups is recommended.

#### *Eutrophication*

With increasing industrial and residential development and settlement of coastal areas, it is expected that sediment and run-off will leach towards the sea.

--- SENTENCE 1181 ---
The steep landscape of American Samoa means that surface water run-off is a lot faster than on more gently-sloping coastlines.

--- SENTENCE 1182 ---
Pollutants, rubbish and nutrients are also washed to the sea where they can have a serious impact on the marine life.

--- SENTENCE 1183 ---
Algal blooms will increase with high nutrients in the environment.

--- SENTENCE 1184 ---
Diminishing diversity of corals and other marine animals is expected.

--- SENTENCE 1185 ---
It is recommended that appropriate measures be undertaken to safeguard the marine environment.

--- SENTENCE 1186 ---
Coupled with these measures is the need to undertaken research in algal population and algal physiology.

#### *Invasive species*

Effective and continuous monitoring is needed to detect and weed out potential invasive species.

--- SENTENCE 1187 ---
This may mean establishing monitoring sites in places where invasive species are likely to be found, such as the inner Pago Pago harbor.

--- SENTENCE 1188 ---
Although only a few relatively less invasive introduced species were found, the consequences of an outbreak by an invader can easily threaten the integrity of the marine environment and the livelihood of the people.

--- SENTENCE 1189 ---
Measures must be taken, including developing appropriate legislation, technology and monitoring regime to keep abreast of invasive species.

--- SENTENCE 1190 ---
#### *Marine plants for food*

Marine plants, especially algae, provide an excellent source of food.

--- SENTENCE 1191 ---
Only two species are commonly eaten in Samoa, although there are probably over 10 seaweed species that can be eaten.

--- SENTENCE 1192 ---
A recipe book on seaweeds, published by the Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC) and the Marine Studies Programme (USP), should be encouraged to promote the consumption of seaweeds by the local people.

--- SENTENCE 1193 ---
The book may need to be translated into the Samoan language to allow for wider audience participation.

--- SENTENCE 1194 ---
#### *Surveys of other sites*

There is a need for marine plant surveys of Tau Island, and

--- SENTENCE 1195 ---
Swains and Rose atolls, as these sites were not surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 1196 ---
Other sites on Tutuila, Ofu, Aunu'u, Olosega islands need to be revisited so that all subtidal and high inter tidal sites are surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 1197 ---
This will include surveys of mangroves and brackish water habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1198 ---
## **5.0 GLOSSARY**

Anastomosing – running together and fusing in such a way that the connections form a reticulation.

--- SENTENCE 1199 ---
Arcuate – curved or bowed.

--- SENTENCE 1200 ---
Carpogonium – the female sex organ in the red algae.

--- SENTENCE 1201 ---
Carposporophyte – a phase of the life history of a red alga, arising directly or indirectly from the carpogonium and borne on the female gametophyte.

--- SENTENCE 1202 ---
At maturity carpospores are produced.

--- SENTENCE 1203 ---
Complanate – strongly flattened and more or less expanded.

--- SENTENCE 1204 ---
Compressed – somewhat flattened.

Dichotomous – forked, with branches more or less of equal size.

--- SENTENCE 1205 ---
Discoid – in a form of a disc.

--- SENTENCE 1206 ---
Distichous - Arranged in two rows on opposite sides of a stem and thus in the same plane.

--- SENTENCE 1207 ---
Flabellate – shaped like a fan.

--- SENTENCE 1208 ---
Flabelloid – fan-like.

Flagellated – provided with a flagellum.

--- SENTENCE 1209 ---
Flagellum – a whip-like part of a motile cell that assists it to move.

--- SENTENCE 1210 ---
Gametophyte – this is the sexual generation of an alga, which is usually represented by a male plant and a female plant.

--- SENTENCE 1211 ---
Membranous – thin and flat, like a membrane.

--- SENTENCE 1212 ---
Midrib – a vein or rib-like structure running up the middle of a blade.

--- SENTENCE 1213 ---
Moniliform – in the form of a string of beads.

--- SENTENCE 1214 ---
Monopodial – a method of branching (see Figure 5).

--- SENTENCE 1215 ---
Mucronate – ending abruptly in a sharp point .

--- SENTENCE 1216 ---
Palmate – diverging in the manner of the fingers from the palm of the hand.

--- SENTENCE 1217 ---
Pinnate – having branches in a rank on either side of the axis (see Figure 5).

--- SENTENCE 1218 ---
Rhizoid – a unicellular or uniseriate root-like filament serving for attachment.

--- SENTENCE 1219 ---
Segmented – divided into segments or joints; articulated.

--- SENTENCE 1220 ---
Segments – the portions of divisions of a thallus between points of branching; or specifically in articulated corallines, the calcified portions (intergenicula) between the articulations.

--- SENTENCE 1221 ---
Siphonous – having siphons.

--- SENTENCE 1222 ---
Solitary – consisting of only one; each one separate by itself.

--- SENTENCE 1223 ---
Spermatia – the non-motile male gametes of the red algae.

--- SENTENCE 1224 ---
Sporophyte – the asexual generation of an alga.

--- SENTENCE 1225 ---
Stipe – stem-like part of a thallus beneath an erect blade.

--- SENTENCE 1226 ---
Stolon - a horizontal branch from the base of plant that produces new plants from buds at its tips

Sympodial – a method of branching (see Figure 5g).

--- SENTENCE 1227 ---
Terete – circular in transverse section.

--- SENTENCE 1228 ---
Tetrasporangium (plural = tetrasporangia) – an asexual reproductive structure in the red algae in which the divisions produce four spores.

--- SENTENCE 1229 ---
Thallus (plural = thalli) – the entire plant body of an alga.

--- SENTENCE 1230 ---
